--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of non-black nodes to remove to disconnect E and J.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of E that could lead to J: M, L, F, B.
Then, I identify all direct neighbors of J that could be reached from E: M, P, I. (Node D is only connected to J and does not lead to E, so it's not relevant for paths from E to J).

Let's analyze the paths from E to J by considering the first node after E:

1.  **Paths starting with E -> M:**
    *   E - M - J
    *   E - L - M - J
    These paths can be blocked by removing node M.

2.  **Paths starting with E -> F:**
    *   E - F - K - G - H - I - J
    *   E - F - X - G - H - I - J
    These paths can be blocked by removing node F.

3.  **Paths starting with E -> B:**
    *   E - B - N - Y - O - P - J
    *   E - B - N - C - O - P - J
    *   E - B - C - O - P - J
    *   E - B - C - I - J
    *   E - B - H - I - J
    These paths can be blocked by removing node B.

Since all paths from E to J must pass through either M, F, or B (the direct neighbors of E that lead towards J), removing these three nodes will disconnect E from J. This gives us a cut set of size 3: {M, F, B}.

To confirm this is the minimum, I need to check if a cut set of size 1 or 2 is possible.

**Checking for a cut set of size 1:**
*   If I remove M: Paths through F (e.g., E-F-K-G-H-I-J) and paths through B (e.g., E-B-C-I-J) remain.
*   If I remove F: Paths through M (e.g., E-M-J) and paths through B (e.g., E-B-C-I-J) remain.
*   If I remove B: Paths through M (e.g., E-M-J) and paths through F (e.g., E-F-K-G-H-I-J) remain.
*   Similarly, checking other central nodes like I or P also leaves other paths open. For example, removing I leaves paths through M (E-M-J) and paths through P (E-B-C-O-P-J) open.
Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

**Checking for a cut set of size 2:**
Let's consider removing any two nodes from the set {M, F, B}:
*   If I remove M and F: Paths starting with E -> B (e.g., E-B-C-I-J) still exist.
*   If I remove M and B: Paths starting with E -> F (e.g., E-F-K-G-H-I-J) still exist.
*   If I remove F and B: Paths starting with E -> M (e.g., E-M-J) still exist.
Therefore, removing any two nodes from {M, F, B} is not sufficient.

Alternatively, let's consider the nodes directly connected to J that are part of paths from E: M, P, I.
*   If I remove M and P: Paths ending in I-J (e.g., E-F-K-G-H-I-J) still exist.
*   If I remove M and I: Paths ending in P-J (e.g., E-B-C-O-P-J) still exist.
*   If I remove P and I: Paths ending in M-J (e.g., E-M-J) still exist.
Therefore, removing any two nodes from {M, P, I} is not sufficient.

Since a cut set of size 1 or 2 is not possible, and I have identified a cut set of size 3 ({M, F, B} or {M, P, I}), the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, F, B]

    *   If a path goes to G via X, it must use the edge X-G.
    *   If a path goes to G via K, it must use the edge K-G.

    *   If I remove only node X, paths from N to G via K would still exist (e.g., N-B-E-F-K-G).
    *   If I remove only node K, paths from N to G via X would still exist (e.g., N-B-E-F-X-G).
    *   Therefore, removing a single node is not sufficient to disconnect N from G.
5.  **Evaluate two node removal:**

6.  **Consider alternative cut sets:**

7.  **Conclusion:** Since a single node removal is insufficient, and a two-node removal (like [X, K] or [F, H]) is sufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

    *   **Path 1:** G - B - N

        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 2 (no common nodes with {H, I, C}).

    Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between G and N, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

4.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects G from N. A straightforward approach is to remove all neighbors of one of the target nodes, except for the other target node itself. In this case, N's neighbors are B, C, and Y. If I remove these three nodes, N will have no connections left to the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from G.

    Let's verify the cut set: **[B, C, Y]**

    Therefore, removing nodes B, C, and Y completely disconnects G from N. This cut set has a size of 3.

5.  **Conclusion:** Since the minimum cut size is at least 3 (due to 3 vertex-disjoint paths) and I have found a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are I and E. I cannot remove these nodes.
2.  **Identify all possible paths between I and E:**
    I is connected to {H, C, P, J}.
    E is connected to {P, L, M, B}.

    Let's list the distinct paths:
    *   **Path 1:** I - P - E
    *   **Path 2:** I - J - M - E
    *   **Path 3:** I - C - B - E
    *   **Path 4:** I - H - C - B - E (This path shares C and B with Path 3)
    *   **Path 5:** I - C - N - O - B - E (This path shares C and B with Path 3)
    *   **Path 6:** I - H - C - N - O - B - E (This path shares H, C, N, O, B with Path 5)
    *   **Path 7:** I - C - N - Y - O - B - E (This path shares C, N, Y, O, B with Path 5)
    *   **Path 8:** I - H - C - N - Y - O - B - E (This path shares H, C, N, Y, O, B with Path 7)
    *   **Path 9:** I - P - J - M - E (This path shares P with Path 1 and J, M with Path 2)

3.  **Look for node-disjoint paths:** To find the minimum number of nodes to remove (the minimum cut set), I can look for the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between I and E (Menger's Theorem).
    *   **Path A:** I - P - E (Nodes: P)
    *   **Path B:** I - J - M - E (Nodes: J, M)
    *   **Path C:** I - C - B - E (Nodes: C, B)

    These three paths (Path A, Path B, Path C) are node-disjoint, meaning they do not share any intermediate nodes.
    *   P is unique to Path A.
    *   J and M are unique to Path B.
    *   C and B are unique to Path C.

    Since I found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect I and E is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** Based on the node-disjoint paths, a cut set can be formed by taking one node from each path.
    Let's try the set [P, J, C].

5.  **Verify the chosen cut set [P, J, C]:**
    If nodes P, J, and C are removed:
    *   **Paths through P:** Path 1 (I-P-E) and Path 9 (I-P-J-M-E) are broken because P is removed.
    *   **Paths through J:** Path 2 (I-J-M-E) is broken because J is removed. Path 9 is also broken.
    *   **Paths through C:** Path 3 (I-C-B-E), Path 5 (I-C-N-O-B-E), Path 7 (I-C-N-Y-O-B-E) are broken because C is removed.
    *   **Paths through H that rely on C:** Path 4 (I-H-C-B-E), Path 6 (I-H-C-N-O-B-E), Path 8 (I-H-C-N-Y-O-B-E) are broken because C is removed.

    Now, let's check if any other paths remain.
    *   From I, the only remaining neighbor is H (C, P, J are removed).
    *   From E, the remaining neighbors are L, M, B (P is removed).

    Can I reach E from I via H?
    I -> H.
    From H, its neighbors are G, I (removed), C (removed).
    So, the only path from H is I -> H -> G.
    From G, its neighbors are H, F, K, X.
    None of F, K, X are connected to E's remaining neighbors (L, M, B).
    Therefore, the path I -> H -> G leads to a dead end for E.

    All paths from I to E are successfully broken by removing P, J, and C.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since we found 3 node-disjoint paths, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G (black) and N (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect G from N.

1.  **Identify the direct connections of the target nodes:**
    *   Node G is directly connected to F, H, K, X.
    *   Node N is directly connected to B, C, Y, O.

2.  **Analyze paths from G to N:** All paths from G to N must start by traversing one of the edges connected to G: (G,F), (G,H), (G,K), or (G,X).

3.  **Evaluate the connectivity of G's neighbors (F, H, K, X):**
    *   **Nodes K and X:**
        *   K is connected to G, F, X.
        *   X is connected to G, F, K.
        *   This means that any path originating from G that goes through K or X (e.g., G-K-... or G-X-...) must eventually pass through F to reach any other part of the graph that could lead to N. For example, G-K-F-... or G-X-F-.... The only other connections for K and X are back to G or to each other, forming local cycles (e.g., G-K-X-G).
    *   **Node F:** F is connected to G, E, M, L, X, K. Through E, M, L, F can lead to B, which connects to N.
    *   **Node H:** H is connected to G, B, I. Through B, H can lead to N. Through I, H can lead to C, J, P, D, O, Y, which can lead to N.

4.  **Consider potential cut sets:**

    *   **Cut set of size 1:**
        *   If we remove any single edge connected to G (e.g., (G,F)), other paths (e.g., G-H-B-N) still exist.
        *   If we remove any single edge connected to N (e.g., (N,B)), other paths (e.g., G-H-I-C-N) still exist.
        *   If we remove any single intermediate edge (e.g., (E,B)), other paths (e.g., G-H-B-N) still exist.
        *   Therefore, a single edge removal is not sufficient to disconnect G and N. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

    *   **Cut set of size 2:**
        Let's consider cutting the "entry points" from G to the rest of the graph.
        *   As established in step 3, any path from G that goes through F, K, or X must eventually pass through F to reach N. Therefore, removing the edge **(G,F)** will effectively cut off all paths from G that would have gone through F, K, or X.
        *   After removing (G,F), the only remaining way for G to connect to the rest of the graph (and thus to N) is through its direct neighbor H, via the edge **(G,H)**.
        *   If we also remove the edge **(G,H)**, then G is completely isolated from the rest of the graph.

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set: [(G,F), (G,H)]**
    *   Removing (G,F) breaks all paths starting G-F-..., G-K-F-..., G-X-F-...
    *   Removing (G,H) breaks all paths starting G-H-...
    *   Since all paths from G to N must start with either G-F, G-H, G-K, or G-X, and the removal of (G,F) covers paths through F, K, and X, and the removal of (G,H) covers paths through H, then G is completely disconnected from N.

6.  **Conclusion:** The cut set [(G,F), (G,H)] consists of 2 edges and successfully disconnects G from N. Since a cut of size 1 is not possible, 2 is the minimum.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(G, F), (G, H)]

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning. The two black nodes are labeled N and G. I will perform a breadth-first search (BFS) starting from node N to find the shortest path to node G.

- **Level 0:** Start node.
  - Queue: `[(N, [N])]`
  - Visited: `{}`

- **Level 1:** Neighbors of N.
  - Pop `(N, [N])`. Add N to Visited.
  - N is connected to B, C, Y.
  - Queue: `[(B, [N, B]), (C, [N, C]), (Y, [N, Y])]`
  - Visited: `{N}`

- **Level 2:** Neighbors of nodes from Level 1.
  - Pop `(B, [N, B])`. Add B to Visited.
    - Neighbors of B: E, H, C (N is visited).
    - Add `(E, [N, B, E])` to queue.
    - Add `(H, [N, B, H])` to queue.
    - C is already in the queue with a shorter path `[N, C]`, so we don't add `[N, B, C]`.
  - Pop `(C, [N, C])`. Add C to Visited.
    - Neighbors of C: I, O, Y (N, B are visited).
    - Add `(I, [N, C, I])` to queue.
    - Add `(O, [N, C, O])` to queue.
    - Y is already in the queue with a shorter path `[N, Y]`, so we don't add `[N, C, Y]`.
  - Pop `(Y, [N, Y])`. Add Y to Visited.
    - Neighbors of Y: O (N, C are visited).
    - O is already in the queue with a path `[N, C, O]`, which is the same length as `[N, Y, O]`. We don't add `[N, Y, O]`.
  - Current Queue: `[(E, [N, B, E]), (H, [N, B, H]), (I, [N, C, I]), (O, [N, C, O])]`
  - Visited: `{N, B, C, Y}`

- **Level 3:** Neighbors of nodes from Level 2.
  - Pop `(E, [N, B, E])`. Add E to Visited.
    - Neighbors of E: L, M, F, G (B is visited).
    - **G is the target node!** Path found: `[N, B, E, G]`. Length: 3.
    Since this is a BFS, this is guaranteed to be the shortest path.

I will verify the path:
1. Is N directly connected to B? Yes, there is a continuous black line.
2. Is B directly connected to E? Yes, there is a continuous black line.
3. Is E directly connected to G? Yes, there is a continuous black line.

The path `[N, B, E, G]` is valid and has a length of 3.

Final Answer: Length: 3, Path: [N, B, E, G]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning. The two black nodes are labeled N and G. I will perform a breadth-first search (BFS) starting from node N to find the shortest path to node G.

1.  **Initialize BFS:**
    *   Queue: `[(N, [N])]` (Node, Path_to_node)
    *   Visited: `{N}`

2.  **Level 1 (Neighbors of N):**
    *   Dequeue `(N, [N])`.
    *   Neighbors of N are Y, B, C.
    *   Add `(Y, [N, Y])`, `(B, [N, B])`, `(C, [N, C])` to the queue.
    *   Visited: `{N, Y, B, C}`
    *   Queue: `[(Y, [N, Y]), (B, [N, B]), (C, [N, C])]`

3.  **Level 2 (Neighbors of Level 1 nodes):**
    *   Dequeue `(Y, [N, Y])`.
        *   Neighbors of Y: O (N is already visited).
        *   Add `(O, [N, Y, O])` to the queue.
    *   Dequeue `(B, [N, B])`.
        *   Neighbors of B: E, H, C (N is already visited, C is already visited).
        *   Add `(E, [N, B, E])` to the queue.
        *   Add `(H, [N, B, H])` to the queue.
            *   **Check H:** H is directly connected to the target node G.
            *   This forms a path: `N → B → H → G`.
            *   The length of this path is 3. Since this is the first time we've reached a neighbor of the target node, and we are using BFS, this is the shortest path.

4.  **Verify the path N → B → H → G:**
    *   Is N directly connected to B? Yes, there is a continuous black line.
    *   Is B directly connected to H? Yes, there is a continuous black line.
    *   Is H directly connected to G? Yes, there is a continuous black line.

The path is valid, and its length is 3. No shorter path can exist because BFS guarantees that the first path found to the target (or a node directly connected to the target) is the shortest.

    *   H is connected to: G, I, C, B.

2.  **Look for edge-disjoint paths:** According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find as many edge-disjoint paths as possible from N to H.

    *   **Path 1:** N - C - H
        Edges: (N, C), (C, H)

    *   **Path 2:** N - B - H
        Edges: (N, B), (B, H)
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as (N, B), (B, H) are distinct from (N, C), (C, H).

    *   **Path 3:** N - O - P - I - H
        Edges: (N, O), (O, P), (P, I), (I, H)
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.
        (N, O) is not (N, C) or (N, B).
        (O, P) is not (C, H) or (B, H).
        (P, I) is not (C, H) or (B, H).
        (I, H) is not (C, H) or (B, H).

    Therefore, there are no more edge-disjoint paths from N to H.

4.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:**
    Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect N from H is at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect N from the rest of the graph (and thus from H) is to remove all edges connecting N to other nodes, except for those leading to isolated parts.
    The edges connecting N to the main graph are (N, C), (N, B), and (N, O).
    If we remove these three edges:
    *   (N, C) is removed.
    *   (N, B) is removed.
    *   (N, O) is removed.
    After these removals, N is only connected to Y. Since Y is only connected to N, N becomes completely isolated from all other nodes in the graph, including H.

6.  **Verify the cut set:**
    The set of edges [(N, C), (N, B), (N, O)] successfully disconnects N from H.
    Since we established that at least 3 edges are required, this set of 3 edges is a minimal cut set.

    *   If we remove the edge (P, D), any path that uses P to reach D will be broken. However, paths that use J to reach D, such as F-E-M-J-D, would still exist.

4.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:**
    Since D has two distinct incoming connections (from J and from P), removing only one edge (either (J, D) or (P, D)) will not disconnect D from F, as the other connection would still provide a path. Therefore, at least two edges must be removed.

5.  **Propose a cut set of size 2:**
    If we remove both edges (J, D) and (P, D), node D will have no remaining connections to the rest of the graph. This means no path can exist from F to D.

6.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   Cut Set: `[(J, D), (P, D)]`
    *   Number of edges: 2
    *   After removing these two edges, D is isolated. All paths from F to D are broken because D itself is no longer connected to any other node.
    *   Since we established that a single edge removal is insufficient, and we found a set of 2 edges that achieves disconnection, this set represents the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D)]

Image name: POGBb_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 18
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N (black) and H (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that, when removed, completely disconnect N from H. This is a minimum cut problem, which by Menger's Theorem, is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between N and H.

I will systematically search for edge-disjoint paths from N to H.

**Path 1:** A direct path through B.
N - B - H
Edges used: `{(N, B), (B, H)}`

**Path 2:** A path through O, P, and I. This path must not use any edges from Path 1.
N - O - P - I - H
Edges used: `{(N, O), (O, P), (P, I), (I, H)}`
These edges are distinct from those in Path 1. So, Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint.

**Path 3:** Now I need to find a third path from N to H that is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.
Edges used so far: `{(N, B), (B, H), (N, O), (O, P), (P, I), (I, H)}`.

1.  From N, the only remaining unused edge is `(N, Y)`. So, Path 3 must start with `N - Y`.
2.  From Y, the only connected node is O. The edge `(Y, O)` is not used in Path 1 or Path 2. So, Path 3 continues `N - Y - O`.
3.  From O, the edges `(N, O)` and `(O, P)` are used in Path 2. The only remaining unused edge from O is `(O, C)`. So, Path 3 continues `N - Y - O - C`.

    *   If we go `C - B`, the edge `(C, B)` is not used in Path 1 or Path 2. So, Path 3 continues `N - Y - O - C - B`.

9.  From G, the only connected node is H. The edge `(G, H)` is not used in Path 1 or Path 2. So, Path 3 completes `N - Y - O - C - B - E - F - X - G - H`.

*   **Path 1:** `(N, B), (B, H)`
*   **Path 2:** `(N, O), (O, P), (P, I), (I, H)`
*   **Path 3:** `(N, Y), (Y, O), (O, C), (C, B), (B, E), (E, F), (F, X), (X, G), (G, H)`

Upon inspection, there are no common edges between any pair of these three paths. Therefore, I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and H.

According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect N and H is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths, which is 3.

- F is connected to G, K, X, P.
- H is connected to G, C, B, I.

Now, let's consider the "entry points" to this G-side cluster from the N-side of the graph.
The nodes on the N-side that connect to H, K, F, or X are:
- To H: C, B, I (via edges (C,H), (B,H), (I,H))
- To F: P (via edge (P,F))
- To K: F, X (but F and X are also part of the G-side cluster, so paths to K from N must go through F or X, or directly to G)
- To X: F, K (similar to K)

Let's try to find a minimal set of edges whose removal would disconnect N from G.

Consider a cut set of size 1:
- If we remove (H,G), paths like N-O-P-F-G still exist.
- If we remove (F,G), paths like N-C-H-G still exist.
- If we remove (K,G), paths like N-O-P-F-G still exist.
- If we remove (X,G), paths like N-O-P-F-G still exist.
- If we remove (P,F), paths like N-C-H-G still exist.
- If we remove (C,H), paths like N-B-H-G still exist.
- If we remove any single edge, a path between N and G remains. Therefore, a cut set of size 1 is not possible.

Consider a cut set of size 2:
Let's analyze the critical connections.
1.  **Paths to G via H**: All paths from N that reach H must eventually use the edge (H,G) to reach G. If we remove (H,G), all paths from N to G that pass through H are broken.
    *   Example paths broken by (H,G): N-C-H-G, N-B-H-G, N-C-I-H-G, N-B-I-H-G, N-O-P-I-H-G.

2.  **Paths to G via F, K, or X**:
    *   Let's look at node F. F is connected to P, K, X, G.
    *   From the N-side of the graph, P is the *only* node connected to F. (P is connected to O, I, J, E, all of which are on the N-side).
    *   Therefore, the edge (P,F) is a critical link. If (P,F) is removed, F becomes disconnected from the N-side of the graph.
    *   Since K and X are only connected to F and G (and each other), if F is disconnected from the N-side, then K and X can only be reached from the N-side by going through G itself (which is what we are trying to prevent).
    *   Thus, removing (P,F) effectively breaks all paths from N to G that pass through F, K, or X.
    *   Example paths broken by (P,F): N-O-P-F-G, N-C-B-E-P-F-G, N-O-P-F-K-G, N-O-P-F-X-G.

By removing the two edges **(H,G)** and **(P,F)**:
- All paths from N to G that would go through H are broken by removing (H,G).
- All paths from N to G that would go through F, K, or X are broken by removing (P,F).

Since all paths from N to G must pass through either H, F, K, or X, and we have successfully cut off all these routes, N and G are completely disconnected.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(H, G), (P, F)]

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 18
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are E and I. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from I. I cannot remove E or I.

2.  **Analyze Connectivity from E:**
    From node E, there are three distinct "entry points" into the rest of the graph:
    *   Via node B
    *   Via node F
    *   Via node L

3.  **Analyze Connectivity to I:**
    Node I is directly connected to H, C, J, and P.

4.  **Search for Node-Disjoint Paths (Menger's Theorem):**
    To determine the minimum cut set size, I will try to find the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between E and I. Node-disjoint paths do not share any intermediate nodes.

    *   **Path 1 (P1): E - B - C - I**
        Intermediate nodes: {B, C}

    *   **Path 2 (P2): E - F - G - H - I**
        Intermediate nodes: {F, G, H}
        Let's check if P1 and P2 are node-disjoint. The set of intermediate nodes for P1 is {B, C}. The set for P2 is {F, G, H}. These sets are disjoint. So, P1 and P2 are node-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3 (P3): E - L - M - J - I**
        Intermediate nodes: {L, M, J}
        Let's check if P3 is node-disjoint from P1 and P2.
        Intermediate nodes for P1: {B, C}
        Intermediate nodes for P2: {F, G, H}
        Intermediate nodes for P3: {L, M, J}
        All three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually disjoint. Therefore, P1, P2, and P3 are three node-disjoint paths between E and I.

5.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:**
    According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes (E and I) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them. Since I found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

6.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 3:**
    Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects E and I. I will look for nodes that act as bottlenecks for the three main branches identified in step 2.

    *   **For paths starting E-B:** All paths starting E-B can be cut by removing node B.
        (e.g., E-B-H-I, E-B-C-I, E-B-N-O-P-I, etc. - all these paths are severed by removing B).

    *   **For paths starting E-F:** All paths from E-F to I must pass through G.
        (E-F-G-H-I, E-F-X-G-H-I, E-F-X-K-G-H-I). From F, you can go to G or X. From X, you can go to G or K. From K, you can go to G. Thus, all paths from F eventually converge on G before proceeding to H and then I. So, removing G will cut all paths originating from E-F.

    *   **For paths starting E-L:** All paths from E-L to I must pass through M.
        (E-L-M-J-I, E-L-M-J-P-I, etc.). From L, you can only go to M. From M, you can only go to J. Thus, all paths from L eventually converge on M before proceeding to J and then I. So, removing M will cut all paths originating from E-L.

    Therefore, removing the set of nodes {B, G, M} will disconnect E from I. This cut set has a size of 3.

7.  **Conclusion:**
    Since I have found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. I have also identified a cut set of size 3 that successfully disconnects E and I. Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, G, M]

        *   H - I - J - P - O - N
        (All paths in this group eventually lead to O, then N.)
    *   **Path Group 3 (via X or G):**
        *   H - X - K - F - E - B - N
        *   H - X - K - F - E - L - M - O - N
        *   H - G - X - K - F - E - B - N
        *   H - G - X - K - F - E - L - M - O - N
        *   H - G - K - F - E - B - N
        *   H - G - K - F - E - L - M - O - N
        (All paths in this group eventually lead to B or O, then N.)

5.  **Identify critical "gateway" nodes to N:**
    Any path from H to N must pass through one of N's direct neighbors (Y, O, B, C).
    *   Node Y is connected to N, but Y is only connected to O. So, any path through Y must first pass through O (e.g., H -> ... -> O -> Y -> N). This means Y is not an independent gateway; its path is covered by O.
    *   Therefore, the effective "gateway" nodes to N are C, O, and B.

6.  **Evaluate single-node removal:**
    *   If we remove **C**: The path H - C - N is broken. However, paths through O (e.g., H - I - J - M - O - N) and paths through B (e.g., H - X - K - F - E - B - N) still exist. So, removing C alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove **O**: All paths through O (e.g., H - I - J - M - O - N, H - X - K - F - E - L - M - O - N) are broken. However, the path H - C - N and paths through B (e.g., H - X - K - F - E - B - N) still exist. So, removing O alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove **B**: All paths through B (e.g., H - X - K - F - E - B - N) are broken. However, the path H - C - N and paths through O (e.g., H - I - J - M - O - N) still exist. So, removing B alone is not enough.
    A single-node cut is not possible.

7.  **Evaluate two-node removal:**
    We need to break all paths that go through C, O, or B.
    *   If we remove **C and O**:
        *   H - C - N is broken (by C).
        *   All paths through O are broken (by O).
        *   However, paths through B (e.g., H - X - K - F - E - B - N) still exist. So, [C, O] is not a cut set.
    *   If we remove **C and B**:
        *   H - C - N is broken (by C).
        *   All paths through B are broken (by B).
        *   However, paths through O (e.g., H - I - J - M - O - N) still exist. So, [C, B] is not a cut set.
    *   If we remove **O and B**:
        *   All paths through O are broken (by O).
        *   All paths through B are broken (by B).
        *   However, the path H - C - N still exists. So, [O, B] is not a cut set.
    A two-node cut is not possible using combinations of C, O, B. Other combinations of two nodes would also leave at least one of these main channels open. For example, removing I and E would still leave H-C-N open.

8.  **Evaluate three-node removal:**
    *   If we remove **C, O, and B**:
        *   H - C - N is broken (by C).
        *   All paths through O are broken (by O).
        *   All paths through B are broken (by B).
        Since all paths from H to N must pass through C, O, or B, removing these three nodes will disconnect H from N.

9.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 3, and the cut set consists of C, O, and B.

The final answer is structured as requested.

Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [C, O, B]

1.  **Identify all direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node E is connected to: M, L, F, B.
    *   Node J is connected to: P, I, D, M.

2.  **Analyze connections from J's perspective:**
    *   Any path from E to J must eventually reach J through one of its direct neighbors.
    *   J is connected to P, I, D, and M.
    *   Node D is a dead-end from E's perspective, meaning there are no paths from E that lead to D and then to J. So, the edge (J, D) does not need to be considered for disconnection from E.
    *   Therefore, all paths from E to J must pass through one of the nodes P, I, or M, and then use the respective edge (P, J), (I, J), or (M, J) to reach J.

3.  **Consider the "exit" edges from the rest of the graph into J:**
    *   Edge (M, J): This edge connects M to J. Paths like E-M-J, E-L-M-J, E-F-G-X-K-M-J use this edge.
    *   Edge (I, J): This edge connects I to J. Paths like E-B-C-I-J, E-B-H-I-J, E-F-G-H-I-J use this edge.
    *   Edge (P, J): This edge connects P to J. Paths like E-B-C-O-P-J use this edge.

4.  **Hypothesize a minimal cut set:**
    To completely disconnect E from J, we must ensure that no path can reach J. Since all paths from E to J must use one of the edges (M, J), (I, J), or (P, J) as their final step, removing these three edges would isolate J from all possible paths originating from E.

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   If we remove (M, J), all paths that go through M to reach J are broken.
    *   If we remove (I, J), all paths that go through I to reach J are broken.
    *   If we remove (P, J), all paths that go through P to reach J are broken.
    *   Since these three edges represent all the "entry points" to J from the E-side of the graph, removing them guarantees that no path exists between E and J.
    *   The size of this cut set is 3.

6.  **Prove minimality (no smaller cut set exists):**
    Let's assume a cut set of size 2 exists, say {e1, e2}.
    *   **Case A: Both e1 and e2 are among {(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)}.**
        *   If we remove (M, J) and (I, J), the path E-B-C-O-P-J is still active (using (P, J)).
        *   If we remove (M, J) and (P, J), the path E-B-C-I-J (or E-B-H-I-J or E-F-G-H-I-J) is still active (using (I, J)).
        *   If we remove (I, J) and (P, J), the path E-M-J (or E-L-M-J or E-F-G-X-K-M-J) is still active (using (M, J)).
        *   In all these subcases, a path remains, so two edges directly connected to J are not sufficient.
    *   **Case B: One edge is directly connected to J (e.g., e1 = (M, J)), and the other edge (e2) is elsewhere in the graph.**
        *   If e1 = (M, J) is removed, all paths through M to J are cut.
        *   Remaining paths to J must go through I or P. Examples: E-B-C-I-J, E-B-H-I-J, E-F-G-H-I-J (through I); E-B-C-O-P-J (through P).
        *   No single edge e2 (e.g., (E, B), (E, F), (B, C), (B, H), (C, I), (C, O), (O, P), (H, I), (F, G), etc.) can simultaneously cut all remaining paths that go through I AND all remaining paths that go through P. For example, if e2 = (E, B), it cuts paths starting E-B, but E-F-G-H-I-J is still active. If e2 = (H, I), it cuts paths E-B-H-I-J and E-F-G-H-I-J, but E-B-C-I-J and E-B-C-O-P-J are still active.
    *   **Case C: Neither e1 nor e2 is directly connected to J.**
        *   This would require finding two edges earlier in the graph that cut all paths. Given the multiple, somewhat independent branches leading to M, I, and P, it's highly unlikely that two such edges exist. For instance, removing (E, B) and (E, F) would still leave paths like E-M-J and E-L-M-J active.

Since a cut set of size 2 is not sufficient, and a cut set of size 3 has been identified, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)]

Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are E and H. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E and H.

2.  **Identify all direct connections to H:** Node H is directly connected to three other nodes: G, I, and B. This means any path from E to H must pass through one of these three nodes (G, I, or B) and then use the edge connecting that node to H.

3.  **Consider a potential cut set by isolating H:** If we remove all edges directly connected to H, H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from E. The edges connected to H are (G, H), (I, H), and (B, H). Removing these three edges would disconnect E and H. This gives us a cut set of size 3: `[(G, H), (I, H), (B, H)]`.

4.  **Determine if this is the minimum number of edges:** To confirm that 3 is the minimum, we can try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum cut size is 3.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths from E to H:
    *   **Path 1:** E - G - H
        Edges: `(E, G)`, `(G, H)`
    *   **Path 2:** E - B - H
        Edges: `(E, B)`, `(B, H)`
    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - P - I - H
        Edges: `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, P)`, `(P, I)`, `(I, H)`

5.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    *   Edges in Path 1: `(E, G)`, `(G, H)`
    *   Edges in Path 2: `(E, B)`, `(B, H)`
    *   Edges in Path 3: `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, P)`, `(P, I)`, `(I, H)`
    Upon inspection, all edges listed across these three paths are unique. There are no shared edges between any two of these paths. Therefore, these are indeed three edge-disjoint paths.

6.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:** Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3. As we found a cut set of size 3 (by removing `(G, H)`, `(I, H)`, and `(B, H)`), this is indeed the minimum.

1.  **Identify all direct neighbors of G and I:**
    *   Neighbors of G: H, K, F, X
    *   Neighbors of I: H, C, P, J

    *   **Path 1 (via H):** G - H - I
        This is a direct path.

    *   **Paths via F:**
        *   G - F - P - I
        *   G - F - P - J - I

    *   **Paths via K:**
        *   G - K - X - F - P - I
        *   G - K - X - F - P - J - I

    *   **Paths via X:**
        *   G - X - F - P - I
        *   G - X - F - P - J - I

3.  **Identify potential bottleneck edges:**
    Let's examine the edges directly connected to node I, as these are the "entry points" to I from the rest of the graph. These edges are (I, H), (I, C), (I, P), and (I, J).

    *   **Can G reach C?**
        Let's check if any of G's neighbors (H, K, F, X) are connected to C, or can lead to C.
        *   H is not connected to C.
        *   K is not connected to C.
        *   F is not connected to C.
        *   X is not connected to C.
        Therefore, there are no paths from G to C. This means the edge (I, C) is not part of any path between G and I, and removing it would not contribute to disconnecting G from I.

    *   **Remaining critical edges connected to I:**
        This leaves us with (I, H), (I, P), and (I, J) as the only edges through which a path from G can reach I.

4.  **Formulate a cut set based on these critical edges:**
    *   The path G - H - I uses the edge (H, I).
    *   All paths from G that go through F, K, or X eventually lead to P. From P, there are two ways to reach I:
        *   Directly via (P, I).
        *   Via J, using (P, J) and then (J, I).

    Therefore, to disconnect G from I, we must cut all these "entry points" to I:
    *   Remove (H, I) to break all paths through H.
    *   Remove (P, I) to break all paths that go directly from P to I.
    *   Remove (J, I) to break all paths that go from P through J to I.

    This gives us a cut set: C = {(H, I), (P, I), (J, I)}. The size of this cut set is 3.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    To confirm that this is the *minimum* number of edges, we need to show that removing any *one* edge from this set would leave at least one path between G and I.

    *   If we remove only {(P, I), (J, I)} (i.e., keep (H, I)): The path G - H - I would still exist.
    *   If we remove only {(H, I), (J, I)} (i.e., keep (P, I)): The path G - F - P - I (and others through F/K/X to P) would still exist.
    *   If we remove only {(H, I), (P, I)} (i.e., keep (J, I)): The path G - F - P - J - I (and others through F/K/X to P-J) would still exist.

    Since removing any single edge from the set C leaves a path between G and I, the set C is a minimal cut set.

Final Answer:

    *   If node E is removed, the connection from F (and thus G) to H and M (which lead to I) is broken. Thus, G would be disconnected from I.
5.  **Determine the minimum number of nodes:** Both F and E are single-node cut sets. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 1. I will choose F as the representative cut set.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G and I.

1.  **Identify direct connections and immediate neighborhoods:**
    *   Node G is connected to: F, X, K, H.
    *   Node I is connected to: J, P, C, H.
    *   Notice that H is a common neighbor, indicating a direct path G-H-I.

2.  **Consider the minimum number of edges:**
    *   **Can it be 1 edge?** No, because G has degree 4 and I has degree 4. Removing any single edge would leave multiple alternative paths. For example, removing (G, H) still leaves paths like G-K-J-I.
    *   **Can it be 2 edges?** This is less obvious. To determine this, I will try to find edge-disjoint paths. If I can find 'k' edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum edge cut is at least 'k'.

3.  **Find edge-disjoint paths between G and I:**
    Let's try to identify paths that do not share any common edges.

    *   **Path 1 (P1):** G - H - I
        Edges: `{(G, H), (H, I)}`

    *   **Path 2 (P2):** G - K - J - I
        Edges: `{(G, K), (K, J), (J, I)}`
        (Check for disjointness with P1: (G,K), (K,J), (J,I) are distinct from (G,H), (H,I). So far, so good.)

    *   **Path 3 (P3):** G - F - M - L - E - B - N - C - I
        Edges: `{(G, F), (F, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, B), (B, N), (N, C), (C, I)}`
        (Check for disjointness with P1 and P2: All edges in P3 are distinct from edges in P1 and P2. For example, (G,F) is distinct from (G,H) and (G,K). (C,I) is distinct from (H,I) and (J,I). All intermediate edges are also distinct.)

    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths (P1, P2, P3) between G and I, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect G and I is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of 3 edges:**
    Now that I know the minimum is at least 3, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects G and I. I can construct a cut set by picking one edge from each of the edge-disjoint paths found above.

    Let's choose:
    *   From P1: (H, I)
    *   From P2: (K, J)
    *   From P3: (B, N) (This edge is a good "bottleneck" for the longer path through F-M-L-E-B-N)

    So, the proposed cut set is `[(H, I), (K, J), (B, N)]`.

5.  **Verify the chosen cut set:**
    I need to ensure that after removing these three edges, no path exists between G and I.

    *   **Removal of (H, I):** This directly breaks the path G-H-I. Any other path that might use H to reach I (e.g., G-H-B-...) would still need to connect to I, but (H,I) is the only direct connection from H to I.
    *   **Removal of (K, J):** This breaks all paths that rely on the connection between K and J.
        *   G-K-J-I is broken.
        *   G-K-J-P-I is broken.
        *   Paths like G-X-K-J-I, G-F-X-K-J-I (and their P-I variants) are also broken because they all pass through K-J.
    *   **Removal of (B, N):** This breaks all paths that rely on the connection between B and N.
        *   Paths like G-F-M-L-E-B-N-C-I, G-F-M-L-E-B-N-O-P-I, G-F-M-L-E-B-N-Y-O-P-I are broken.
        *   Similarly, paths starting G-X-F-M-L-E-B-N... are also broken.
        *   Paths starting G-K-X-F-M-L-E-B-N... are also broken.

    By removing these three edges, all identified major routes from G to I are severed.
    *   Paths through H are cut by (H, I).
    *   Paths through K and J are cut by (K, J).
    *   Paths through F/X/K leading to M-L-E-B-N and then to C/P are cut by (B, N).

    Since we have found a cut set of size 3, and we proved that at least 3 edges are required, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The goal is to find the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect the two black nodes, H and N, without removing H or N themselves. This is equivalent to finding the minimum vertex cut between H and N. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum vertex cut size is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

First, I will identify vertex-disjoint paths between H and N. Vertex-disjoint means that these paths do not share any intermediate nodes (nodes other than H and N).

1.  **Path 1:** H - B - N
    *   Intermediate node: {B}

2.  **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
    *   Intermediate nodes: {I, C}

3.  **Path 3:** H - E - M - J - P - O - N
    *   Intermediate nodes: {E, M, J, P, O}

Let's check if the sets of intermediate nodes for these three paths are disjoint:
*   Set 1: {B}
*   Set 2: {I, C}
*   Set 3: {E, M, J, P, O}

These three sets are indeed disjoint. Since we have found three vertex-disjoint paths between H and N, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Next, I need to demonstrate that removing 3 nodes is sufficient to disconnect H and N. I will choose one node from each of the disjoint sets of intermediate nodes identified above to form a cut set. A suitable cut set would be [B, I, E].

Let's verify that removing nodes B, I, and E disconnects H and N:
1.  **Removing B:** This severs the direct path H - B - N.
2.  **Removing I:** This severs all paths that pass through I.
    *   Paths like H - I - C - N, H - I - P - O - N, H - I - J - P - O - N are broken.
    *   Paths like H - E - I - C - N, H - E - I - P - O - N, H - E - I - J - P - O - N are also broken.
3.  **Removing E:** This severs all paths that pass through E.
    *   Paths like H - E - M - J - P - O - N, H - E - L - M - J - P - O - N are broken.
    *   Any paths that might have gone H - E - G - F - ... are also broken from reaching N, as G's only connection to N's side (E) is now removed.

After removing B, I, and E:
*   Node H is now only connected to node G.
*   Node G is connected to F, K, X, and H. None of F, K, or X are connected to N or any nodes that can reach N.
*   Therefore, H is completely disconnected from N.

Since we established that at least 3 nodes must be removed, and we found a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects H and N, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, I, E]

    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N

    *   (B, N)
    *   (C, N)
    *   (Y, N)

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B and H, both represented as black circles with white labels. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between B and H.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are B and H.

2.  **List all direct neighbors of H:**
    *   H is connected to B (edge (B, H)).
    *   H is connected to G (edge (G, H)).
    *   H is connected to I (edge (I, H)).
    These are the only three edges directly connected to node H.

3.  **Analyze paths to H:** Any path from node B to node H must, by definition, use one of these three edges as its final segment to reach H.
    *   Path 1: B directly to H via edge (B, H).
    *   Path 2: B to G, then G to H via edge (G, H). (Example: B-E-F-G-H)
    *   Path 3: B to I, then I to H via edge (I, H). (Example: B-C-I-H)

4.  **Determine a cut set:** To completely disconnect B from H, all possible paths between them must be broken. Since all paths to H must pass through one of the three edges (B, H), (G, H), or (I, H), removing these three edges will ensure that H is isolated from B and the rest of the graph.
    Therefore, the set of edges {(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)} is a valid cut set. The size of this cut set is 3.

5.  **Verify minimality:** Now, I need to check if a smaller cut set (e.g., of size 1 or 2) could also disconnect B and H.
    *   **Size 1:** Removing any single edge (e.g., (B, H)) would leave other paths intact (e.g., B-E-F-G-H and B-C-I-H). So, a single edge is not enough.
    *   **Size 2:** Let's consider removing any two edges from the identified set:
        *   If I remove (B, H) and (G, H), the path B-C-I-H still exists.
        *   If I remove (B, H) and (I, H), the path B-E-F-G-H still exists.
        *   If I remove (G, H) and (I, H), the direct path B-H still exists.
    Since removing any two of these three edges leaves at least one path between B and H, a cut set of size 2 is not sufficient.

6.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of edges that must be removed is 3, and the cut set consists of the three edges directly connected to node H.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

1.  **Identify all direct neighbors of H and N:**
    *   Neighbors of H: E, G, B, I
    *   Neighbors of N: B, C, O, Y

2.  **Identify potential "bottleneck" nodes or edges:**
    Any path from H to N must eventually pass through one of N's direct neighbors (excluding Y, as Y is not connected to any node that H can reach without passing through N's other neighbors). The relevant neighbors of N are B, C, and O.

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths:**
    According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from H to N.

    *   **Path 1 (P1): H - B - N**
        Edges: `(H, B)`, `(B, N)`

    *   **Path 2 (P2): H - I - C - N**
        Edges: `(H, I)`, `(I, C)`, `(C, N)`
        (Check for disjointness with P1: No common edges. P1 uses B, P2 uses I and C.)

    *   **Path 3 (P3): H - E - M - J - P - O - N**
        Edges: `(H, E)`, `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, P)`, `(P, O)`, `(O, N)`
        (Check for disjointness with P1 and P2:
        - P3 uses E, M, J, P, O. P1 uses B. P2 uses I, C. No common edges with P1 or P2.)

    Since we have found three completely edge-disjoint paths between H and N, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    To confirm that 3 edges are sufficient, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects H from N. A straightforward approach is to cut all edges directly connected to N, excluding (N, Y) since Y is not part of any path from H to N.

    Consider the edges: `(N, B)`, `(N, C)`, `(N, O)`.
    *   Removing `(N, B)` breaks Path 1 (H - B - N).
    *   Removing `(N, C)` breaks Path 2 (H - I - C - N).
    *   Removing `(N, O)` breaks Path 3 (H - E - M - J - P - O - N). It also breaks any other path that ends at N via O, such as H - I - P - O - N or H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N.

    Since all paths from H to N must pass through B, C, or O immediately before reaching N, removing the edges `(N, B)`, `(N, C)`, and `(N, O)` will disconnect N from all possible paths originating from H.

    This cut set has a size of 3.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. We also found a cut set of size 3. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Let's categorize the paths based on their initial connection from F:

**Category 1: Paths starting F -> K**
The only path segment from K that leads towards D is K-M-J-D.
So, paths in this category are: F-K-M-J-D.
To cut this path, we could remove (F, K), (K, M), (M, J), or (J, D).

**Category 2: Paths starting F -> X or F -> G**
Both X and G are connected to H.
Paths from F via X or G lead to H:
- F-X-G-H-...
- F-G-H-...
From H, paths can go to I, and then to J or P, which connect to D.
- H-I-J-D
- H-I-P-D
So, paths in this category include: F-X-G-H-I-J-D, F-X-G-H-I-P-D, F-G-H-I-J-D, F-G-H-I-P-D.
A critical edge for all these paths is (G, H).

**Category 3: Paths starting F -> E**
From E, there are two main branches:
- Branch 3a: E -> L -> M -> J -> D. This path segment uses (M, J).
- Branch 3b: E -> B -> ... (This branch leads to H, C, N, I, O, which then connect to D).
  - E-B-H-I-J-D

  - E-B-N-C-O-Y-D
A critical edge for all paths in Branch 3b is (E, B).

Let's define a conceptual cut that separates F from D.
Consider the set of nodes S = {F, K, X, G, E, M, L} (the "left" side containing F).
Consider the set of nodes V-S = {H, I, J, P, D, B, C, N, O, Y} (the "right" side containing D).

The edges that cross this cut (i.e., connect a node in S to a node in V-S) are:
1. **(G, H)**: Connects G (in S) to H (in V-S). This edge is crucial for all paths from F that go through G or X and then H.
2. **(E, B)**: Connects E (in S) to B (in V-S). This edge is crucial for all paths from F that go through E and then B.
3. **(M, J)**: Connects M (in S) to J (in V-S). This edge is crucial for all paths from F that go through K-M or E-L-M and then J.

Let's verify that removing these three edges [(G, H), (E, B), (M, J)] disconnects F from D:
- All paths from F via K (e.g., F-K-M-J-D) are broken by removing (M, J).
- All paths from F via X or G (e.g., F-X-G-H-I-J-D, F-G-H-I-P-D) are broken by removing (G, H).
- All paths from F via E and then B (e.g., F-E-B-C-O-D, F-E-B-H-I-J-D) are broken by removing (E, B).
- All paths from F via E-L-M (e.g., F-E-L-M-J-D) are broken by removing (M, J).

Since these three edges cover all possible routes from F to D, their removal ensures complete disconnection. This gives us a cut set of size 3.

Now, I need to prove that this is the *minimum* number of edges, meaning no cut set of size 2 exists. I will do this by showing that if any two of these three edges are removed, a path between F and D still exists.

    - Paths via M-J are broken.
    - However, paths via G-H are still intact. For example, F-X-G-H-I-J-D still exists. Therefore, F and D are still connected.

Since removing any two of these three edges leaves at least one path between F and D, a cut set of size 2 is not possible. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are E and H.
2.  **Identify direct connections:** There is a direct edge between E and H, labeled (E, H). To disconnect E and H, this edge must be removed.
3.  **Analyze paths after removing (E, H):** After removing the direct edge (E, H), we need to find all alternative paths from E to H.
    *   Node E is connected to F, L, and M (besides H).
    *   Node H is connected to G, B, and I (besides E).
    *   Therefore, any remaining path from E to H must start from E, go through F, L, or M, traverse other nodes, and eventually reach H through G, B, or I.

4.  **Identify critical "entry points" to H:** All paths from any node in the graph to H (excluding the direct (E,H) path) must pass through one of H's remaining neighbors: G, B, or I. This means all such paths must use one of the edges (G, H), (B, H), or (I, H) as their final step to reach H.

5.  **List representative paths (after removing (E,H)):**
    *   **Path 1 (via G):** E - F - G - H. This path uses the edge (G, H).
    *   **Paths (via I):**
        *   E - L - M - J - I - H
        *   E - L - M - J - P - I - H
        *   E - L - M - J - D - P - I - H
        *   E - M - J - I - H
        *   E - M - J - P - I - H
        *   E - M - J - D - P - I - H
        All these paths use the edge (I, H).
    *   **Paths (via B):**
        *   E - L - M - J - P - C - B - H
        *   E - L - M - J - D - P - C - B - H
        *   E - M - J - P - C - B - H
        *   E - M - J - D - P - C - B - H
        All these paths use the edge (B, H).

6.  **Determine the minimum cut set:**
    *   We must remove (E, H). (1 edge)
    *   To cut all remaining paths, we must ensure that no path can reach H through G, B, or I.
    *   Consider the three edges (G, H), (B, H), and (I, H). These are the only remaining connections to H from the rest of the graph.
    *   If we remove only one of these three edges (e.g., (G, H)), paths through (B, H) and (I, H) would still exist.
    *   If we remove any two of these three edges (e.g., (G, H) and (B, H)), paths through the remaining edge (I, H) would still exist (e.g., E - L - M - J - I - H).
    *   Therefore, to completely disconnect H from all other nodes (and thus from E), we must remove all three edges: (G, H), (B, H), and (I, H). (3 edges)

7.  **Calculate total edges:** The total number of edges to remove is 1 (for (E, H)) + 3 (for (G, H), (B, H), (I, H)) = 4 edges.
    This set of edges [(E, H), (G, H), (B, H), (I, H)] isolates H from the rest of the graph, ensuring no path exists between E and H. This is a minimal cut because we've demonstrated that removing fewer than 3 edges from the set {(G, H), (B, H), (I, H)} (in addition to (E, H)) would leave at least one path.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(E, H), (G, H), (B, H), (I, H)]

Node H is connected to: E, F, G, B, I.

I can categorize the paths between E and H into three main groups based on their immediate connections:
1.  **Direct Path:** E - H
2.  **Paths through common neighbors F and G (the "left" cluster):**
    *   E - F - H
    *   E - G - H
    *   (And longer paths through K, X, but these must pass through F or G to connect to E or H).
3.  **Paths through other neighbors (the "right/bottom" cluster):**
    *   Paths starting from E via L or M, and ending at H via B or I. Examples:
        *   E - L - M - J - I - H
        *   E - M - J - I - H
        *   E - L - M - J - P - I - H
        *   E - M - J - P - I - H

        *   E - M - J - P - C - B - H
        *   (And longer paths through N, O, Y, D, but these must pass through L/M to connect to E, and B/I to connect to H).

To disconnect E and H, I must ensure that no path from any of these three categories remains.

**Attempt to find a cut set of size 1, 2, or 3:**
*   **Size 1:** Removing any single edge (e.g., (E,H)) leaves multiple other paths (e.g., E-F-H, E-G-H, E-M-J-I-H). So, 1 edge is not enough.
*   **Size 2:** Removing two edges (e.g., (E,H) and (E,F)) still leaves paths like E-G-H and E-M-J-I-H. So, 2 edges are not enough.
*   **Size 3:**
    *   Consider removing the direct path and the two paths through common neighbors: [(E,H), (E,F), (E,G)]. After removing these, E is connected to L, M. H is connected to F, G, B, I. Paths like E-M-J-I-H still exist. So, 3 edges are not enough.
    *   Consider removing the direct path and the other two edges to common neighbors: [(E,H), (F,H), (G,H)]. After removing these, E is connected to F, G, L, M. H is connected to B, I. Paths like E-M-J-I-H still exist. So, 3 edges are not enough.

Since 3 edges are not enough, the minimum cut must be at least 4.

**Attempt to find a cut set of size 4:**
Let's assume we remove 4 edges.
*   **Scenario A: The direct edge (E,H) is NOT removed.**
    In this case, we need to cut all other paths using 4 edges.
    *   If we remove all edges connecting E to its non-H neighbors: [(E,F), (E,G), (E,L), (E,M)]. After this, E is only connected to H. The path E-H still exists. This is not a cut.
    *   Similarly, if we remove all edges connecting H to its non-E neighbors: [(H,F), (H,G), (H,B), (H,I)]. After this, H is only connected to E. The path E-H still exists. This is not a cut.
    This implies that if (E,H) is not removed, we need more than 4 edges to disconnect E and H.

*   **Scenario B: The direct edge (E,H) IS removed.** (1 edge used)
    We have 3 more edges to cut all remaining paths:
    *   Paths through F and G (e.g., E-F-H, E-G-H).
    *   Paths through L/M and B/I (e.g., E-M-J-I-H).

    Therefore, 4 edges are not enough.

Since 4 edges are not enough, the minimum cut must be at least 5.

**Constructing a cut set of size 5:**
Let's try to cut the direct path, all paths from E to the "left" cluster, and all paths from H to the "right" cluster.
Consider the following set of 5 edges:
1.  **(E,H)**: Cuts the direct path.
2.  **(E,F)**: Cuts all paths from E to F.
3.  **(E,G)**: Cuts all paths from E to G.
4.  **(H,B)**: Cuts all paths to H via B.
5.  **(H,I)**: Cuts all paths to H via I.

Let's verify this cut set: [(E,H), (E,F), (E,G), (H,B), (H,I)].
*   (E,H) is removed, so no direct path.
*   (E,F) is removed, so E cannot reach F. Paths like E-F-H are broken.
*   (E,G) is removed, so E cannot reach G. Paths like E-G-H are broken.
*   (H,B) is removed, so H cannot be reached via B. Paths like E-M-J-P-C-B-H are broken.
*   (H,I) is removed, so H cannot be reached via I. Paths like E-M-J-I-H are broken.

After removing these 5 edges:
*   E is now only connected to L and M.
*   H is now only connected to F and G (but E cannot reach F or G anymore due to (E,F) and (E,G) being removed).
*   Any path from E to H would have to go from E (via L or M) to some node in the "right" cluster (J, P, C, N, O, Y, D) and then eventually to H. However, the only connections from this "right" cluster to H are (H,B) and (H,I), both of which have been removed.
Therefore, E and H are completely disconnected.

This cut set of 5 edges successfully disconnects E and H. Since we've established that 4 edges are not enough, 5 is the minimum.

The target nodes are B (black) and M (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between B and M.

*   Neighbors of B: E, H, N, C
*   Neighbors of M: F, L

1.  **Paths starting from B through E:**
    *   B - E - F - M
    *   B - E - L - M

2.  **Paths starting from B through H:**
    *   B - H - G - F - M
    *   B - H - G - X - F - M

3.  **Paths starting from B through N or C:**
    *   I examine paths through N: N is connected to B, C, Y.
        *   N -> C -> I -> J -> D (D is not connected to M)
        *   N -> C -> O -> P -> J -> D (D is not connected to M)
        *   N -> Y -> O -> P -> J -> D (D is not connected to M)

    *   I examine paths through C: C is connected to B, N, I, O.
        *   C -> I -> J -> D (D is not connected to M)
        *   C -> O -> P -> J -> D (D is not connected to M)
        *   No paths from C lead to M.

Therefore, all paths from B to M must pass through either node E or node H from B's perspective, and must pass through node F or node L to reach M.

To disconnect B and M, I need to break all these identified paths.

**Strategy 1: Cut edges directly connected to M.**
*   M is connected to F via (F, M) and to L via (L, M).
*   If I remove (F, M), paths B-E-F-M, B-H-G-F-M, and B-H-G-X-F-M are broken. However, the path B-E-L-M still exists.
*   If I remove (L, M), the path B-E-L-M is broken. However, paths B-E-F-M, B-H-G-F-M, and B-H-G-X-F-M still exist.
*   Therefore, to disconnect M from all incoming paths, both (F, M) and (L, M) must be removed.
*   This results in a cut set of size 2: [(F, M), (L, M)].

**Strategy 2: Cut edges directly connected from B that lead to M.**
*   B has connections to E and H that lead to M.
*   If I remove (B, E), paths B-E-F-M and B-E-L-M are broken. However, paths B-H-G-F-M and B-H-G-X-F-M still exist.
*   If I remove (B, H), paths B-H-G-F-M and B-H-G-X-F-M are broken. However, paths B-E-F-M and B-E-L-M still exist.
*   Therefore, to disconnect B from all paths leading to M, both (B, E) and (B, H) must be removed.
*   This results in a cut set of size 2: [(B, E), (B, H)].

**Strategy 3: Mixed approach.**
*   Consider removing (B, E). This breaks paths B-E-F-M and B-E-L-M.
*   The remaining paths are B-H-G-F-M and B-H-G-X-F-M. To break these, I could remove (H, G) or (G, F) or (G, X) or (X, F) or (F, M). For example, removing (H, G) would break both remaining paths.
*   This results in a cut set of size 2: [(B, E), (H, G)].

In all these scenarios, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect B and M is 2. It's impossible to disconnect them by removing only one edge, as there are at least two independent "bottlenecks" (e.g., the two edges directly connected to M, or the two edges leading out of B towards M).

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, M), (L, M)]

    *   Nodes directly connected to E are: B, F, L.

3.  **Identify a Potential Cut Set:**

If nodes B, F, and L are removed, E will have no remaining connections to any other node. Therefore, H will be completely disconnected from E. This forms a cut set of size 3: {B, F, L}.

4.  **Verify Minimum Cut Size using Menger's Theorem:**
    To confirm that 3 is the *minimum* number of nodes, I will find the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between H and E. According to Menger's theorem, this number will be equal to the minimum vertex cut size. Two paths are vertex-disjoint if they do not share any intermediate nodes (nodes other than H and E).

    Let's find vertex-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - I - B - E
        Intermediate nodes used: {I, B}

    *   **Path 2:** H - C - O - P - J - M - L - E
        Intermediate nodes used: {C, O, P, J, M, L}
        This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1, as {C, O, P, J, M, L} does not overlap with {I, B}.

    *   **Path 3:** H - X - F - E
        Intermediate nodes used: {X, F}
        This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2, as {X, F} does not overlap with {I, B} or {C, O, P, J, M, L}.

    I have found three vertex-disjoint paths. Let's check if a fourth vertex-disjoint path exists.
    The remaining direct neighbor of H is G.
    The remaining direct neighbors of E are B, F, L (but these are already used as entry points in the paths above).
    Any path starting H - G must reach E without using any of the intermediate nodes from P1, P2, P3 ({I, B, C, O, P, J, M, L, X, F}).
    *   Consider H - G - K - F - E. This path uses F, which is an intermediate node in Path 3. So, it is not vertex-disjoint.
    *   Consider H - G - X - F - E. This path uses X and F, which are intermediate nodes in Path 3. So, it is not vertex-disjoint.
    All paths from H through G eventually lead to F (H-G-F-E or H-G-K-F-E or H-G-X-F-E or H-G-X-K-F-E). Since F is already used in Path 3, no additional vertex-disjoint path can be formed through G.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since there are 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and E, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is 3. The cut set {B, F, L} successfully disconnects H from E and has a size of 3.

        *   Can N reach D? No, D is only connected to J (which is removed) and P.
        *   Since N can still reach O (which is connected to P), [I, J] is not a cut set.

    *   **If I remove O and J:**
        *   Paths to P via O are broken.
        *   Paths to P via J are broken.
        *   Remaining direct connections to P: I, D.
        *   Can N reach I? Yes, e.g., N -> C -> I.
        *   Can N reach D? No, D is only connected to J (which is removed) and P.
        *   Since N can still reach I (which is connected to P), [O, J] is not a cut set.

    *   **If I remove C and J:**
        *   Paths from N through C are broken.
        *   Paths to P via J are broken.
        *   Remaining direct connections to P: I, O, D.
        *   Can N reach I? Yes, e.g., N -> B -> E -> G -> H -> I.
        *   Can N reach O? Yes, e.g., N -> Y -> O.
        *   Can N reach D? No, D is only connected to J (which is removed) and P.
        *   Since N can still reach I and O (which are connected to P), [C, J] is not a cut set.

    Based on these attempts, it appears that 2 nodes are not sufficient.

5.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Vertex-Disjoint Paths):**
    To confirm the minimum number of nodes, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between N and P (excluding N and P themselves). If I can find *k* vertex-disjoint paths, then the minimum vertex cut size is at least *k*.

    Let's identify three vertex-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** N - Y - O - P
        *   Nodes (excluding N, P): {Y, O}
    *   **Path 2:** N - C - I - P
        *   Nodes (excluding N, P): {C, I}
    *   **Path 3:** N - B - E - L - M - J - P
        *   Nodes (excluding N, P): {B, E, L, M, J}

    Let's check for disjointness:
    *   Set 1: {Y, O}
    *   Set 2: {C, I}
    *   Set 3: {B, E, L, M, J}
    These three sets of nodes are completely disjoint. Therefore, there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths between N and P. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect N and P must be at least 3.

6.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    Since the minimum is at least 3, I will try to find a cut set of size 3. Consider the direct neighbors of P: I, O, J, D. If I remove I, O, and J:
    *   All paths directly entering P via I, O, or J are broken.
    *   The only remaining direct connection to P is D.
    *   Can N reach D? D is only connected to J and P. Since J is removed, D is isolated from the rest of the graph (except P). Therefore, N cannot reach D.
    *   Thus, removing {I, O, J} successfully disconnects N from P.

7.  **Conclusion:**
    I have found a cut set of size 3 ([I, O, J]), and I have proven using Menger's Theorem that the minimum cut size must be at least 3. Therefore, 3 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

The final answer is:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [I, O, J]

        *   Therefore, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

                    *   B -> N. N's neighbors are B, C, O, Y. None of these lead to D.
        *   Since D is not reachable from E after removing I, M, and P, the set {I, M, P} successfully disconnects E from J. This is a cut set of size 3.

    *   **Size 2:** We need to check all combinations of two nodes from {I, M, P} (or any other two nodes).

        *   Removing {I, P}: Paths like E - M - J still exist (using M).
        *   Removing {M, P}: Paths like E - I - J still exist (using I).
        Since removing any two nodes from {I, M, P} leaves at least one path, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

7.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 3, and the cut set is {I, M, P}.

Edges connected to B: (B, H), (B, C), (B, N), (B, O), (B, E) - a total of 5 edges.

If I remove all 5 edges connected to B, then B becomes an isolated node, and no path can exist between H and B. This gives a cut set of size 5: [(B, H), (B, C), (B, N), (B, O), (B, E)]. Similarly, removing all 5 edges connected to H would also disconnect H from B.

Now, I need to determine if a smaller cut (e.g., of size 4 or less) exists.
Let's consider the set of edges directly connected to B: $E_B = \{(B, H), (B, C), (B, N), (B, O), (B, E)\}$.
Suppose we try to remove only 4 edges from $E_B$. This means at least one edge from $E_B$ will remain. Let this remaining edge be $(B, X)$, where X is one of H, C, N, O, or E. For the two black nodes to remain connected, there must be a path from H to X that does not use any of the 4 removed edges.

Let's test this for each possible remaining edge:
1.  **If (B, H) remains:** There is a direct path H-B. This path is not broken.
2.  **If (B, C) remains:** We need to check if H can reach C without using the other 4 edges from $E_B$.
    *   Path: H-C. This path exists. So, H-C-B would be a path.
3.  **If (B, N) remains:** We need to check if H can reach N without using the other 4 edges from $E_B$.
    *   Path: H-C-N. This path exists. So, H-C-N-B would be a path.
4.  **If (B, O) remains:** We need to check if H can reach O without using the other 4 edges from $E_B$.
    *   Path: H-C-O. This path exists. So, H-C-O-B would be a path.
    *   Another path: H-I-P-O. This path also exists. So, H-I-P-O-B would be a path.
5.  **If (B, E) remains:** We need to check if H can reach E without using the other 4 edges from $E_B$.
    *   Path: H-G-F-E. This path exists. So, H-G-F-E-B would be a path.
    *   Another path: H-X-F-E. This path exists. So, H-X-F-E-B would be a path.
    *   Another path: H-I-J-M-L-E. This path exists. So, H-I-J-M-L-E-B would be a path.

In every scenario where 4 edges from $E_B$ are removed, there is always at least one remaining edge $(B, X)$ and a path from H to X that does not rely on the other edges in $E_B$. Therefore, removing any 4 edges directly connected to B is insufficient to disconnect H from B.

This proves that at least 5 edges must be removed to disconnect H and B. Since we have identified a cut set of 5 edges (all edges connected to B), this is the minimum number.

3.  **Initial Assessment (Upper Bound):**
    *   Node N has three direct connections: (N, Y), (N, O), (N, B). Removing these three edges would isolate N, thus disconnecting it from G. This gives an upper bound of 3 edges.
    *   Node G has three direct connections: (G, H), (G, K), (G, X). Removing these three edges would isolate G, thus disconnecting it from N. This also gives an upper bound of 3 edges.
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of edges is at most 3.

4.  **Attempt to find 3 Edge-Disjoint Paths (to prove min-cut >= 3):**
    Let's try to find three paths from N to G that do not share any edges.
    *   **Path 1 (P1):** N - B - H - G
        Edges: {(N,B), (B,H), (H,G)}
    *   **Path 2 (P2):** N - O - P - D - J - M - E - F - K - G
        Edges: {(N,O), (O,P), (P,D), (D,J), (J,M), (M,E), (E,F), (F,K), (K,G)}
        P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint.
    *   **Attempt to find Path 3 (P3) disjoint from P1 and P2:**
        Let's try starting from N-Y.
        N - Y - O - C - I - H - G
        This path uses (H,G), which is part of P1. Thus, it is not edge-disjoint from P1.
        Let's try another branch from N-Y-O-C.
        N - Y - O - C - B - E - L - M.
        From M, the only connections are (M,J), (M,E), (M,L).
        (M,J) is in P2. (M,E) is in P2. (M,L) is not in P1 or P2.
        So, N - Y - O - C - B - E - L - M. From M, we can only go to L.
        N - Y - O - C - B - E - L - M - L (loop). This path gets stuck at M or L without using edges from P1 or P2 to reach G.
        Let's re-examine connections from E: E is connected to B, L, M, F.
        (E,M) and (E,F) are in P2. (E,B) and (E,L) are not in P1 or P2.
        So, from N-Y-O-C-B-E, we can only go to L.
        This systematic search suggests that it's difficult to find a third edge-disjoint path. This hints that the minimum cut might be 2.

5.  **Search for a Cut Set of Size 2:**
    If the minimum cut is 2, we need to find two edges whose removal disconnects N and G.
    Let's analyze the "exit points" from the N-side and "entry points" to the G-side.
    The nodes directly connected to G are H, K, and X. All paths to G must pass through one of these nodes.

    Consider removing the edge (H,G). This breaks all paths that directly enter G via H.
    Now, all remaining paths from N to G must go through K or X.
    Let's examine how paths reach K or X:
    *   K is connected to F, X, G.
    *   X is connected to F, K, G.
    Notice that both K and X are connected to F.
    Any path from the "N-side" to F must pass through E (F is connected to E, K, X).
    Therefore, any path from N to K or X (and subsequently to G) must pass through the edge (E,F).

        *   Path: E - M - J
        *   Path: E - L - M - J
        To cut all paths in this branch, we must remove either the edge (E, M) or the edge (M, J).

        *   E - H - I - J
        *   E - H - I - P - J
        *   E - H - I - P - D - J

        *   E - F - G - H - ...
        *   E - G - H - ...
        To cut all paths in this branch, the most efficient way is to remove the edge (E, H).

4.  **Formulate a minimal cut set:**
    To completely disconnect E from J, we need to cut both of these main branches.
    *   From Branch 1 (paths through M), we choose to remove edge (M, J). This breaks E-M-J and E-L-M-J.
    *   From Branch 2 (paths through H), we choose to remove edge (E, H). This breaks all paths originating from E that pass through H (including those that go E-F-G-H or E-G-H).

    *   Any path E -> M -> J is broken by removing (M, J).

    *   Any path E -> H -> ... -> J is broken by removing (E, H).

    *   Any path E -> G -> H -> ... -> J is broken by removing (E, H).

    *   Can we disconnect E and J by removing only 1 edge? No. If we remove (M, J), paths like E-H-I-J still exist. If we remove (E, H), paths like E-M-J still exist. Therefore, at least 2 edges are required.

7.  Since 2 edges are required and we found a set of 2 edges that achieves disconnection, this is the minimum number.

    *   **Path Group 1 (via M):**
        *   P1: E - M - J
        *   P2: E - L - M - J

    *   **Path Group 2 (via F):**

        *   P5: E - F - G - H - I - J

        *   P9: E - F - G - B - N - Y - O - P - J

        *   P12: E - B - N - C - O - P - J
        *   P13: E - B - N - Y - O - P - J

    *   **Path A:** E - M - J (Intermediate node: M)
    *   **Path B:** E - F - K - J (Intermediate nodes: F, K)
    *   **Path C:** E - B - H - I - J (Intermediate nodes: B, H, I)

    These three paths (A, B, C) are node-disjoint because their intermediate nodes (M; F, K; B, H, I) are all distinct.
    According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect E and J is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them. Since I found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.

4.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects E and J.
    Consider the immediate neighbors of E: L, M, F, B.
    Any path from E to J must pass through at least one of these nodes (or M directly).

    *   Removing **F** disconnects all paths starting with E-F (P3, P4, P5, P6, P7, P8, P9).
    *   Removing **B** disconnects all paths starting with E-B (P10, P11, P12, P13).

    Since all initial branches from E (E-L, E-M, E-F, E-B) are severed by removing {M, F, B}, E becomes completely disconnected from J.

5.  **Conclusion:** I have found 3 node-disjoint paths, which implies a minimum cut set size of at least 3. I have also found a cut set of size 3 ({M, F, B}) that successfully disconnects E and J. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

    *   Paths starting M-J can go through I (M-J-I-...) or P (M-J-P-...).
    *   Paths starting M-E (or M-L-E) can go through H (M-E-H-...) or M-E-G-H-... (which also passes through H).
    Therefore, to disconnect M from N, we must sever all connections from M's "side" of the graph to these three critical nodes (H, I, P).
3.  **Identify the "M-side" of the graph:** This includes M and all nodes reachable from M without passing through H, I, or P. These nodes are {M, E, L, J, G, F, K, X}.
4.  **Identify all edges connecting the "M-side" to the critical nodes (H, I, P):**
    *   Edges connecting to H:
        *   (E, H): Node E is in the M-side.
        *   (G, H): Node G is in the M-side (reachable via M-E-G).
    *   Edges connecting to I:
        *   (J, I): Node J is in the M-side.
    *   Edges connecting to P:
        *   (J, P): Node J is in the M-side.
    The set of these edges is S = {(E, H), (G, H), (J, I), (J, P)}.
5.  **Verify that removing these edges disconnects M and N:**
    If we remove all edges in S:
    *   M can no longer reach H via E (due to (E, H) removal) or via G (due to (G, H) removal).
    *   M can no longer reach I via J (due to (J, I) removal).
    *   M can no longer reach P via J (due to (J, P) removal).
    Since M cannot reach H, I, or P, and all paths to N must pass through one of these nodes, M is completely disconnected from N. The size of this cut set is 4.
6.  **Prove that this is the minimum number of edges (i.e., 3 edges are not enough):**
    To show that 4 is the minimum, I must demonstrate that removing any *three* edges from the set S will still leave at least one path between M and N.
    *   **Case 1: Remove (E, H), (G, H), (J, I).**
        The edge (J, P) remains. A path M - J - P - O - N still exists.

    *   **Case 3: Remove (E, H), (J, I), (J, P).**
        The edge (G, H) remains. A path M - E - G - H - B - N still exists.
    *   **Case 4: Remove (G, H), (J, I), (J, P).**
        The edge (E, H) remains. A path M - E - H - B - N still exists.
    In all cases where 3 edges are removed from S, a path between M and N still exists. Therefore, 3 edges are not sufficient to disconnect M and N.

Conclusion: The minimum number of edges to remove is 4.
