Let's consider the immediate neighbors of H: G, B, I.

All paths from E to H must pass through one of E's neighbors and eventually reach one of H's neighbors.

I can identify two main "bundles" of paths from E to H:

**Bundle 1: Paths through node B**
These paths start from E, go to B, and then proceed to H. Examples include:
*   E - B - H
*   E - B - I - H
*   E - B - C - I - H
*   E - B - N - C - I - H (and longer variations)

To disconnect all paths in this bundle, I need to remove an edge that is common to all of them. The edge **(E, B)** is the first edge for all these paths from E. If I remove (E, B), all paths starting E-B-... are broken.

**Bundle 2: Paths through nodes F, M, L, X, and G**
These paths start from E, go through F, M, or L, and eventually converge to G before reaching H. Examples include:
*   E - F - G - H
*   E - F - X - G - H
*   E - M - F - G - H
*   E - M - F - X - G - H
*   E - L - M - F - G - H
*   E - L - M - F - X - G - H

All these paths, regardless of whether they start E-F, E-M, or E-L, eventually lead to node G (either directly from F, or via F-X). From G, the only way to reach H is through the edge **(G, H)**. Therefore, removing the edge (G, H) will break all paths in this bundle.

**Proposed Cut Set:**
Based on this analysis, if I remove the edge **(E, B)** (to cut Bundle 1) and the edge **(G, H)** (to cut Bundle 2), both E and H should be completely disconnected from each other.

**Verification:**
1.  **Removing (E, B):** This edge directly connects E to B. Any path from E that starts by going to B (e.g., E-B-H, E-B-I-H, E-B-C-I-H) will be broken.
2.  **Removing (G, H):** This edge directly connects G to H. Any path from E that does not use (E, B) must start with (E, F), (E, M), or (E, L).
    *   If a path starts E-F-..., it must eventually reach G (via F-G or F-X-G) to get to H. Removing (G, H) breaks this connection.
    *   If a path starts E-M-..., it must go M-F-... and then through G to reach H. Removing (G, H) breaks this connection.
    *   If a path starts E-L-..., it must go L-M-F-... and then through G to reach H. Removing (G, H) breaks this connection.

Since all paths from E to H fall into either Bundle 1 or Bundle 2, and both bundles are severed by removing (E, B) and (G, H) respectively, E and H will be completely disconnected.

**Minimum Number of Edges:**
To confirm this is the minimum, I consider if a single edge removal could disconnect E and H.
*   If I remove (E, B), paths like E-F-G-H still exist.
*   If I remove (G, H), paths like E-B-H still exist.
*   If I remove (H, B), paths like E-B-I-H and E-F-G-H still exist.
*   If I remove (H, I), paths like E-B-H and E-F-G-H still exist.
Since there are at least two edge-disjoint paths (e.g., E-B-H and E-F-G-H), a single edge removal is insufficient. Therefore, the minimum number of edges required is 2.

    *   From Path 3: B

First, I identify all edges directly connected to node H:
1.  (B, H)
2.  (G, H)
3.  (I, H)

Any path from B to H must traverse one of these three edges to reach H. If all three of these edges are removed, node H will become isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from node B. This would result in a cut set of size 3: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)].

Now, I need to determine if a smaller cut set (e.g., of size 1 or 2) is possible. To do this, I will look for edge-disjoint paths between B and H. If I can find `k` edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least `k`.

Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from B to H:
1.  **Path 1:** B - H
    *   Edges: {(B, H)}

2.  **Path 2:** B - I - H
    *   Edges: {(B, I), (I, H)}
    *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it uses different edges.

3.  **Path 3:** B - E - F - G - H
    *   Edges: {(B, E), (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}
    *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 (no (B,H)) and Path 2 (no (B,I) or (I,H)).

I have found three distinct paths that do not share any common edges:
*   P1: (B, H)
*   P2: (B, I), (I, H)
*   P3: (B, E), (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)

Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and H, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

The cut set identified earlier, [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)], consists of 3 edges.
*   Removing (B, H) breaks Path 1.
*   Removing (I, H) breaks Path 2 (and any other path going through I to H, like B-C-I-H, B-N-C-I-H, B-C-O-P-I-H).
*   Removing (G, H) breaks Path 3 (and any other path going through G to H, like B-E-L-M-F-G-H).

After removing these three edges, node H has no remaining connections to any other node in the graph, thus ensuring it is completely disconnected from B. Since the minimum number of edges required is 3, and I have found a cut set of 3 edges, this is the minimal solution.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

    *   If we remove any other single edge in the graph (e.g., (M, J), (I, J), (O, P), (C, P), (I, P), or any edge further away from D), there will always be alternative paths to both J and P, and thus to D. For example, if (M, J) is removed, paths through I-J-D still exist, and paths to P-D still exist.
    *   Therefore, a single edge removal is insufficient to disconnect F from D.
7.  **Conclusion:** Since a cut of size 1 is not possible, and we have found a cut of size 2 that successfully disconnects F from D, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2. The cut set [(J, D), (P, D)] achieves this.

The final answer is $\boxed{\text{Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D)]}}$

Let's identify vertex-disjoint paths from G to I:

*   **Path 1:** G - H - I
    *   Intermediate node: {H}

*   **Path 2:** G - X - K - J - P - I

*   **Path 3:** G - F - E - B - C - I

*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {H}.
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {X, K, J, P}.
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {F, E, B, C}.

These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually disjoint. This means we have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between G and I.
According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect G and I is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3.

    *   If C is removed, Path 3 (G - F - E - B - C - I) is broken.

Since these three paths are vertex-disjoint and each node in the cut set {H, P, C} breaks one of these paths, and removing these three nodes isolates I from the rest of the graph, this cut set successfully disconnects G and I.

Since we found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3.
Since we found a cut set of size 3 ({H, P, C}), the minimum cut size is exactly 3.

    *   F is connected to X, K, G.
    *   X is connected to F, K, G.

    *   G is connected to F, X, K.

    *   (F, E) - connects F to E.
    *   (G, H) - connects G to H.

3.  **Propose a cut set:**

4.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   If (F, E) is removed, F can no longer reach E directly.
    *   If (G, H) is removed, G can no longer reach H directly.

    *   With (G, H) removed, no path can go F -> (X or K or G) -> G -> H -> ... -> J.
    *   Therefore, removing both (F, E) and (G, H) effectively disconnects F from J.

5.  **Check for minimality:**

        *   If only (F, E) is removed, paths like F-G-H-I-J still exist.
        *   If only (G, H) is removed, paths like F-E-J still exist.
        *   If any other single edge is removed (e.g., (E, J), (I, J), etc.), there will always be alternative paths between F and J.
    *   Since 1 edge is not sufficient, and we found a cut set of 2 edges, this set is minimal.

After removing (E, M) and (J, M), node M is only connected to L. Since L is not connected to F, M is completely disconnected from F.
This set of edges [(E, M), (J, M)] successfully disconnects F and M. The size of this cut set is 2.

Let's verify if there are other minimal cut sets of size 2.
Another option is to cut the "entry points" from F: (F, E) and (F, G).
- Removing (F, E) breaks the path F-E-M.
- Removing (F, G) breaks all paths starting with F-G (e.g., F-G-H-E-M, F-G-H-I-J-M, etc.).
This set of edges [(F, E), (F, G)] also disconnects F and M, and its size is 2.

Since I have found a cut set of size 2, and I've established that 1 edge is not enough, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, M), (J, M)]

1.  **Identify direct connections of the target nodes:**
    *   Node F is connected to X, K, G, and E.
    *   Node D is connected to J and P.

2.  **Analyze paths to D:**
    Any path from F to D must eventually reach D. Looking at node D, it has only two incoming edges: (J, D) and (P, D). This means that any path from F to D must necessarily pass through either node J or node P, and then use the edge (J, D) or (P, D) respectively.

3.  **Consider minimum cut size:**
    *   **Can we disconnect F and D by removing 1 edge?**
        If we remove only (J, D), paths from F to P and then to D (e.g., F-E-I-P-D) would still exist.
        If we remove only (P, D), paths from F to J and then to D (e.g., F-E-I-J-D) would still exist.
        Therefore, removing a single edge is not sufficient to disconnect F from D.

    *   **Can we disconnect F and D by removing 2 edges?**
        If we remove both edges (J, D) and (P, D), node D will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Since D has no other connections, no path from F (or any other node) can reach D. This successfully disconnects F from D.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    Since 1 edge is not enough and 2 edges are sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2. The cut set consists of the two edges directly connected to D.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D)]

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 18
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are E and J.
2.  **Identify direct connections to J from the rest of the graph:** Node J is directly connected to M, K, I, P, and D. Node D is not connected to E or any node that E can reach, so the edge (D, J) is not part of any path between E and J. Therefore, any path from E to J must pass through one of the nodes M, K, I, or P.
3.  **Consider the edges connecting these "gateway" nodes to J:** These edges are (M, J), (K, J), (I, J), and (P, J). If we remove all these four edges, J will be completely disconnected from M, K, I, and P. Since all paths from E to J must pass through one of these four nodes, removing these four edges will disconnect E from J. This gives a potential cut set of size 4: `[(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)]`.

4.  **Determine if a smaller cut set exists (e.g., size 3):** To prove that 4 is the minimum, we can try to find 4 edge-disjoint paths between E and J. If we can find 4 such paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 4.

    Let's identify four edge-disjoint paths from E to J:
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J
        Edges: `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - K - J
        Edges: `(E, F)`, `(F, K)`, `(K, J)`
    *   **Path 3:** E - G - H - I - J
        Edges: `(E, G)`, `(G, H)`, `(H, I)`, `(I, J)`
    *   **Path 4:** E - B - C - O - P - J
        Edges: `(E, B)`, `(B, C)`, `(C, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, J)`

5.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    *   The first edges from E are `(E, M)`, `(E, F)`, `(E, G)`, `(E, B)`. These are all distinct.
    *   The last edges into J are `(M, J)`, `(K, J)`, `(I, J)`, `(P, J)`. These are all distinct.
    *   Let's check all intermediate edges:
        *   `(F, K)` is only in Path 2.
        *   `(G, H)` and `(H, I)` are only in Path 3.
        *   `(B, C)`, `(C, O)`, and `(O, P)` are only in Path 4.
    All edges used in these four paths are unique to their respective paths. Therefore, these are indeed four edge-disjoint paths.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since there are 4 edge-disjoint paths from E to J, at least 4 edges must be removed to disconnect them. We have also identified a cut set of 4 edges `[(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)]` that achieves this disconnection. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)]

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are M and B.
2.  **Analyze connections from M:** Node M is directly connected to L, F, and E.
3.  **Analyze connections to B:** Node B is directly connected to N, H, C, and E.
4.  **Identify common nodes/bottlenecks:**
    *   Notice that node E is directly connected to both M and B (M-E-B). This is a direct path.
    *   All other paths from M to B must go through L or F from M's side, and eventually reach H, C, or N on B's side.
5.  **Categorize paths:** I can group all paths from M to B into two main categories:
    *   **Category 1: Paths that use node E.** These include the direct path M-E-B, and paths like M-L-E-B and M-F-E-B.
    *   **Category 2: Paths that do NOT use node E.** These paths must go through F, then G, then H, to reach B. Examples include M-L-F-G-H-B, M-F-G-H-B, and variations involving K or X (e.g., M-L-F-K-G-H-B, M-F-X-G-H-B). All these paths share the sequence F -> G -> H.
6.  **Determine the minimum cut:**
    *   To break all paths in Category 1, node E must be removed.
    *   To break all paths in Category 2, at least one node from the sequence F -> G -> H must be removed. Removing F, G, or H would sever all these paths.
    *   Since these two categories of paths are distinct (one uses E, the other does not), we need to remove at least one node from each "bottleneck" to ensure complete disconnection.
7.  **Form a cut set:**
    *   Removing E takes care of Category 1.
    *   Removing F (or G, or H) takes care of Category 2.
    *   Therefore, a set of two nodes, such as [E, F], [E, G], or [E, H], will disconnect M and B.
8.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   A single node removal is not sufficient. For example, removing E leaves paths like M-F-G-H-B. Removing F leaves paths like M-E-B. Removing G leaves paths like M-E-B. Removing H leaves paths like M-E-B.
    *   Since a single node is not enough, and we found a 2-node cut set, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node B is connected to: C, N, O, E, I.
    *   Node H is connected to: C, I, G.

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    A straightforward approach is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes.
    *   To isolate H, we would remove edges (H, C), (H, I), and (H, G). This is a cut of size 3.
    *   To isolate B, we would remove edges (B, C), (B, N), (B, O), (B, E), and (B, I). This is a cut of size 5.
    The smaller of these two options is 3. This gives us an upper bound for the minimum cut.

3.  **Explore paths between B and H to confirm the minimum:**
    To ensure that 3 is indeed the minimum, we can look for edge-disjoint paths between B and H. If we can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum edge cut must be at least *k*.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from B to H:
    *   **Path 1:** B - C - H
        Edges: (B, C), (C, H)
    *   **Path 2:** B - I - H
        Edges: (B, I), (I, H)
    *   **Path 3:** B - E - P - F - G - H
        Edges: (B, E), (E, P), (P, F), (F, G), (G, H)

    Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   Path 1 uses (B, C) and (C, H).
    *   Path 2 uses (B, I) and (I, H).
    *   Path 3 uses (B, E), (E, P), (P, F), (F, G), (G, H).
    All edges in these three paths are distinct. Therefore, these are 3 edge-disjoint paths.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut:**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and H, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.
    We found a cut set of size 3 by removing all edges connected to H: [(C, H), (I, H), (G, H)].
    This set of edges successfully disconnects H from the rest of the graph, and thus from B.
    Since we found a cut of size 3 and established that at least 3 edges are required, 3 is the minimum number of edges to remove.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(C, H), (I, H), (G, H)]

Image name: FD-FR_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 18
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are H and B. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from B.

2.  **Understand the Problem:** This is a minimum cut problem. According to Menger's Theorem (edge version), the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes. Therefore, I will first try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between H and B.

3.  **Identify Edge-Disjoint Paths:** I will systematically search for paths from H to B, ensuring that each new path does not use any edge already used by a previously identified path.

    *   **Path 1 (P1):** H - B
        Edges used: `{(H, B)}`

    *   **Path 2 (P2):** H - E - B
        Edges used: `{(H, E), (E, B)}`
        These edges are distinct from P1's edges.

    *   **Path 3 (P3):** H - I - B
        Edges used: `{(H, I), (I, B)}`
        These edges are distinct from P1's and P2's edges.

    *   **Path 4 (P4):** I need to find a path from H to B that does not use any of the edges from P1, P2, or P3: `(H, B), (H, E), (E, B), (H, I), (I, B)`.
        Let's try to start from H via (H, G) or (H, F) and find a route to B that avoids the used edges.
        Consider the path: H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - C - B
        Edges used: `{(H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, C), (C, B)}`
        Let's check if any of these edges overlap with P1, P2, or P3:
        -   `P1: (H, B)` - No overlap.
        -   `P2: (H, E), (E, B)` - No overlap.
        -   `P3: (H, I), (I, B)` - No overlap.
        Therefore, P4 is edge-disjoint from P1, P2, and P3.

4.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** Since I have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between H and B, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and B is at least 4.

5.  **Identify a Minimal Cut Set:** Now I need to find a set of 4 edges whose removal actually disconnects H and B.
    Consider the following set of edges: `[(H, B), (H, I), (H, E), (F, E)]`.
    Let's verify if removing these edges disconnects H from B:
    *   Removing `(H, B)` breaks the direct connection between H and B.
    *   Removing `(H, I)` ensures H cannot reach node I directly.
    *   Removing `(H, E)` ensures H cannot reach node E directly.
    *   Removing `(F, E)` ensures that even if H reaches F (via `(H, F)` or `(H, G)-(G, F)`), it cannot then proceed to E.

    After removing these 4 edges, H is only connected to G and F.
    *   From G, H can reach F, K, X.
    *   From F, H can reach G, K, X.
    None of the nodes {G, F, K, X} are connected to B, I, or E (the primary gateways to B from the "left" side of the graph) after the specified edges are removed. Specifically, H cannot reach I or E because `(H, I)` and `(H, E)` are removed. Furthermore, H cannot reach E via F because `(F, E)` is removed. Since B's remaining connections are to C, N, I, E, and H cannot reach I or E, and cannot reach C or N without passing through I or E, H is completely disconnected from B.

6.  **Conclusion:** A cut set of 4 edges has been identified, and it has been proven that at least 4 edges are required to disconnect H and B. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(H, B), (H, I), (H, E), (F, E)]

M's direct neighbors are L, E, F, J.
B's direct neighbors are E, G, H, C, N.

I will systematically explore paths and identify potential bottlenecks.

Let's try to find vertex-disjoint paths between M and B. If I can find `k` vertex-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least `k`.

Here are three vertex-disjoint paths (meaning they do not share any intermediate nodes):
1.  **Path 1:** M - E - B
    *   Intermediate node: {E}
2.  **Path 2:** M - F - G - B
    *   Intermediate nodes: {F, G}
    *   This path is disjoint from Path 1 as {F, G} does not intersect {E}.
3.  **Path 3:** M - J - P - O - C - B
    *   Intermediate nodes: {J, P, O, C}
    *   This path is disjoint from Path 1 as {J, P, O, C} does not intersect {E}.
    *   This path is disjoint from Path 2 as {J, P, O, C} does not intersect {F, G}.

Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between M and B, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 nodes that actually disconnects M and B.

Consider the following set of nodes: [J, E, G].
Let's verify if removing these three nodes disconnects M from B:

1.  **Remove J:** All paths starting with M -> J -> ... -> B are broken.
    *   Examples: M-J-I-C-B, M-J-I-N-B, M-J-P-I-C-B, M-J-P-I-N-B, M-J-P-O-C-B, M-J-P-O-N-B.

    *   Note that L is only connected to E from M's side, so removing E also effectively isolates L from B.

    *   Examples: M-F-G-B, M-F-K-G-B, M-F-X-K-G-B, M-F-G-H-B, M-F-G-H-I-C-B, M-F-G-H-I-N-B.

*   M cannot reach E (E is removed).

Since all direct connections from M (L, E, F, J) are either removed or lead to removed nodes, M is completely disconnected from B.

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt2_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 18
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are G (black) and N (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from N.

1.  **Identify the target nodes' direct connections:**
    *   Node G is directly connected to H, F, E, X.
    *   Node N is directly connected to B, C, Y.

2.  **Consider a simple strategy: Isolate one of the target nodes.**
    *   To isolate node N, all edges connected to it must be removed.
    *   The edges connected to N are (N, B), (N, C), and (N, Y).
    *   If these three edges are removed, N becomes an isolated node, and thus no path can exist between G and N.
    *   This gives a potential cut set of size 3: `[(N, B), (N, C), (N, Y)]`.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut set (e.g., size 2) is possible.**
    To prove that 2 edges are not sufficient, we need to show that there are at least three "edge-disjoint" paths (or paths that cannot be simultaneously cut by just two edges) between G and N.

    Let's identify distinct paths from G to N, focusing on their final connections to N:
    *   **Path 1 (via B):** G - E - B - N
        *   (Note: Other paths to B exist, e.g., G-F-E-B-N, G-H-I-C-B-N, etc., but (B,N) is the final edge.)
    *   **Path 2 (via C):** G - H - I - C - N
        *   (Note: Other paths to C exist, e.g., G-H-I-O-C-N, G-X-K-J-P-O-C-N, etc., but (C,N) is the final edge.)
    *   **Path 3 (via Y):** G - H - I - O - Y - N
        *   (Note: Other paths to Y exist, e.g., G-X-K-J-P-O-Y-N, etc., but (Y,N) is the final edge.)

    These three paths (P1, P2, P3) are distinct in their final edge to N: (B,N), (C,N), and (Y,N).
    *   If we remove any two of these three final edges (e.g., (N,B) and (N,C)), the third path (P3 via (N,Y)) would still exist.
    *   Therefore, removing only two edges directly connected to N is not sufficient to disconnect G from N.

    Now, let's consider if two edges *not* directly connected to N could disconnect G from N.
    Any path from G to N must eventually pass through one of the nodes B, C, or Y. Let's consider the edges that lead into this {B, C, Y} cluster from the rest of the graph (excluding N itself):
    *   Edges leading to B: (E, B), (C, B)
    *   Edges leading to C: (I, C), (O, C), (B, C)
    *   Edges leading to Y: (O, Y)

    Let's try to find two edges whose removal would cut all paths.
    Consider the following three "bottleneck" edges that represent distinct routes into the {B, C, Y} cluster:
    *   (E, B) - This is a primary entry point to B.
    *   (I, C) - This is a primary entry point to C.
    *   (O, Y) - This is the *only* entry point to Y from the rest of the graph (excluding N).

    If we remove any two of these three edges, at least one path will remain:
    *   If we remove (E, B) and (I, C):
        *   Paths to Y via (O, Y) still exist (e.g., G-H-I-O-Y-N).
        *   Paths to C via (O, C) still exist (e.g., G-H-I-O-C-N).
        *   Paths to B via (C, B) still exist (e.g., G-H-I-O-C-B-N).
    *   This demonstrates that removing two edges is not sufficient.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    Since 3 edges are sufficient (by isolating N) and 2 edges are not sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3. The simplest cut set is to remove all edges directly connected to node N.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, Y)]

12. G - H - E - M - J - P - O - N (This path also uses E)

*   If I remove node F: Paths 1-6 are broken. However, paths 7-12 (which start G -> H) still exist, so G remains connected to N.
*   If I remove node H: Paths 7-12 are broken. However, paths 1-6 (which start G -> F) still exist, so G remains connected to N.

*   If F is removed, all paths that start with G -> F (paths 1-6) are broken.
*   If H is removed, all paths that start with G -> H (paths 7-12) are broken.

Thus, removing nodes F and H successfully disconnects G from N. This is a cut set of size 2. Since we've established that a cut set of size 1 is not possible, 2 is the minimum number of nodes.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [F, H]

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are G and N. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect G from N. I cannot remove G or N.

2.  **Explore paths from G to N:** I will systematically list paths to understand the graph's structure between G and N.
    *   Paths from G can start via F, H, K, or X. Nodes X and K are connected to G and F, so paths through them will eventually merge with paths through F or H. Thus, all paths from G to N must effectively pass through either F or H at some point.

3.  **Identify potential bottlenecks (cut nodes):** To find the minimum number of nodes, I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of vertices in a vertex cut is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between the two nodes. I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between G and N (excluding G and N themselves).

    Let's try to find three vertex-disjoint paths:

    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I - C - N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {H, I, C}

    *   **Path 2:** G - F - E - B - N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {F, E, B}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1, as {F, E, B} and {H, I, C} are distinct sets of nodes.

    *   **Path 3:** G - K - D - J - P - O - N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {K, D, J, P, O}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2, as {K, D, J, P, O} is distinct from {H, I, C} and {F, E, B}.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut size:** Since I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between G and N, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect G and N is 3.

5.  **Identify a cut set:** To disconnect G and N, I need to remove at least one intermediate node from each of these three vertex-disjoint paths.
    *   From Path 1 ({H, I, C}), I can choose I.
    *   From Path 2 ({F, E, B}), I can choose B.
    *   From Path 3 ({K, D, J, P, O}), I can choose P.

    Therefore, the set {B, I, P} should disconnect G and N.

6.  **Verify the chosen cut set {B, I, P}:**
    *   **Removing B:** Cuts all paths that go through B to N. This includes G-F-E-B-N, G-H-B-N, and G-F-K-D-J-I-H-B-N.
    *   **Removing I:** Cuts all paths that go through I. This includes G-H-I-C-N, G-F-K-D-J-I-C-N, G-H-I-J-P-O-N, G-H-I-P-O-N, G-F-K-D-J-I-P-O-N, and G-F-K-D-J-I-H-B-N.
    *   **Removing P:** Cuts all paths that go through P to O and then N. This includes G-F-K-D-J-P-O-N, G-F-K-D-J-I-P-O-N, G-H-I-J-P-O-N, and G-H-I-P-O-N.

    By removing B, I, and P, all identified paths from G to N are severed. Since we found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, a cut set of size 3 is minimal.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, I, P]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J. This is a minimum edge cut problem. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

1.  **Identify direct connections for E and J:**
    *   Neighbors of E: F, L, M, I, B
    *   Neighbors of J: M, D, P, I

2.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths from E to J:**
    I will try to find as many edge-disjoint paths as possible.

    *   **Path 1:** A straightforward path through M.
        E - M - J
        Edges: {(E, M), (M, J)}

    *   **Path 2:** A straightforward path through I.
        E - I - J
        Edges: {(E, I), (I, J)}
        Let's check if Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint.
        (E, M) is not (E, I). (M, J) is not (I, J). These two paths are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3:** Now I need to find a third path that does not use any of the edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
        From E, I can go to F, L, or B.
        From J, I can be reached from D or P (besides M and I which are already covered).
        Let's try to reach J via P.
        E - B - C - O - P - J
        Edges: {(E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)}
        Let's check if Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.
        - Path 3 vs Path 1: (E, B) is not (E, M). (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J) are not (M, J). No common edges.
        - Path 3 vs Path 2: (E, B) is not (E, I). (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J) are not (I, J). No common edges.
        So, Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are all edge-disjoint.

3.  **Determine the minimum cut size:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

4.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    To disconnect J from E, I can remove all edges connecting J to the rest of the graph, except for those that are not part of a path from E.
    The direct neighbors of J are M, D, P, I.
    If I remove the edges (J, M), (J, I), and (J, P), then J will only be connected to D.
    Let's verify if E can reach D. D is connected only to J and P. Since (J, P) is removed, P cannot reach J. And E cannot reach D directly or through any other path without going through J or P. Therefore, D is effectively isolated from E.
    Thus, removing [(J, M), (J, I), (J, P)] will disconnect E from J. This cut set has 3 edges.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum number of edges to remove.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(J, M), (J, I), (J, P)]

        *   Removing F breaks all paths that start G-F.

    *   **Option 2: Remove H and E.**

Image name: PG_task3_gt2_PAIR1

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect the two black nodes, N and G.

First, I identify the two target nodes: N (top-middle) and G (bottom-middle).

Next, I consider the direct connections to G: (G, X), (G, K), and (G, H). If all three of these edges are removed, node G becomes isolated, and thus disconnected from N. This would be a cut set of size 3: [(G, X), (G, K), (G, H)]. This provides an upper bound for the minimum number of edges.

Now, I need to determine if a smaller cut (e.g., of size 1 or 2) is possible.
A cut of size 1 would mean there is a single bridge edge whose removal disconnects N and G. By visual inspection, there are clearly multiple paths between N and G, so a cut of size 1 is not possible.

A cut of size 2 would mean that all paths between N and G must pass through a specific pair of edges. To prove that a cut of size 2 is not possible, I would ideally need to find three edge-disjoint paths between N and G (by Menger's Theorem). However, finding edge-disjoint paths in a complex graph by visual inspection can be challenging and prone to error. Instead, I will systematically explore potential cut sets of size 2 and demonstrate that they fail to disconnect N and G.

Let's analyze the graph structure to identify potential "bottleneck" areas.
Node N has connections to B, O, and Y (Y connects only to O, so N-Y-O is effectively N-O).
Node G has connections to H, X, and K.

All paths from N to G must eventually pass through some set of edges that bridge the "N-side" of the graph to the "G-side". Let's consider the edges that connect the upper/middle part of the graph (containing N, B, O, C, I, J, M, L, P, D, Y, E) to the lower part (containing G, H, X, K, F).

The critical edges that connect these two regions, leading towards G, appear to be:
1. Edges leading to the F-K-X cluster: (E, F)
2. Edges leading to the H-I-C cluster: (I, H), (C, H)

Let's test combinations of 2 edges for a cut set:

Attempt 1: Remove (E, F) and (H, G).
- Removing (E, F) breaks all paths that go through E-F (e.g., N-B-E-F-K-G, N-O-M-L-E-F-X-G).
- After removing (E, F), paths like N-B-C-H-G, N-B-C-H-X-G, N-B-C-I-H-G, N-O-M-J-I-H-G, N-O-P-J-I-H-G still exist.
- If we then remove (H, G), paths like N-B-C-H-G, N-B-C-I-H-G, N-O-M-J-I-H-G, N-O-P-J-I-H-G are broken.
- However, paths like N-B-C-H-X-G (using H-X then X-G) still exist.
Therefore, [(E, F), (H, G)] is not a cut set.

Attempt 2: Remove (E, F) and (X, G).
- Removing (E, F) breaks paths through E-F.
- After removing (E, F), paths like N-B-C-H-G, N-B-C-H-X-G, N-B-C-I-H-G, N-O-M-J-I-H-G, N-O-P-J-I-H-G still exist.
- If we then remove (X, G), paths like N-B-C-H-X-G are broken.
- However, paths like N-B-C-H-G (using H-G directly) still exist.
Therefore, [(E, F), (X, G)] is not a cut set.

Attempt 3: Remove (I, H) and (C, H).
- Removing (I, H) breaks paths like N-B-C-I-H-G, N-O-M-J-I-H-G, N-O-P-J-I-H-G.
- After removing (I, H), paths like N-B-E-F-K-G, N-B-C-H-G, N-O-M-L-E-F-X-G still exist.
- If we then remove (C, H), paths like N-B-C-H-G are broken.
- However, paths like N-B-E-F-K-G (using E-F) still exist.
Therefore, [(I, H), (C, H)] is not a cut set.

Since I cannot find a cut set of size 2, and I have identified a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove must be 3.

    *   Can H reach L? Yes, for example: H - G - K - L.

    *   Similarly, if any other set of four neighbors is removed, the remaining fifth neighbor would still provide a path to E, and H can reach that neighbor.
7.  Therefore, removing four nodes is not sufficient to disconnect H and E.
8.  To ensure complete disconnection, all five of E's direct neighbors must be removed. This set is {M, F, B, C, L}.
9.  Removing these five nodes will isolate E from the rest of the graph, thus ensuring no path exists between H and E.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 5, Cut Set: [M, F, B, C, L]

Step-by-step reasoning:

1.  **Identify all direct connections to J:** Node J is connected to four other nodes: K, I, P, and D. Therefore, there are four edges directly connected to J: (K, J), (I, J), (P, J), and (D, J).

2.  **Consider the effect of removing these edges:** If we remove all four of these edges, J will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph. This would certainly disconnect E from J. This gives us an upper bound for the minimum number of edges: 4.

3.  **Check for minimality (can we do it with fewer than 4 edges?):** To prove that 4 is the minimum, we need to demonstrate that removing any set of 3 edges is insufficient to disconnect E from J.
    Let's consider the four edges directly connected to J: `S_J = {(K, J), (I, J), (P, J), (D, J)}`. Any path from E to J must use one of these four edges as its final step.

    Suppose we remove any three edges from `S_J`. For example, let's remove `(K, J)`, `(I, J)`, and `(P, J)`.
    After removing these three edges, the edge `(D, J)` still remains.
    Now, we need to check if there is still a path from E to D (without using the removed edges).
    Let's trace a path from E to D:
    E - B - N - O - P - D.
    This path exists and does not use any of the removed edges `(K, J)`, `(I, J)`, or `(P, J)`.

    Since the path E - B - N - O - P - D exists, and the edge `(D, J)` was not removed, the complete path E - B - N - O - P - D - J still exists after removing three edges from `S_J`.

    This demonstrates that removing any three edges from the set of edges directly connected to J is not sufficient to disconnect E from J. Since all paths to J must use one of these four edges, and we've shown that removing any three leaves a path, the minimum number of edges required must be at least 4.

4.  **Conclusion:** Since removing 4 edges (all direct connections to J) disconnects E from J, and we've proven that 3 edges are not sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

    *   Edge (L, M)
    *   Edge (L, E)

    *   Edge (H, G)
    *   Edge (H, B)
    *   Edge (H, I)

3.  **Consider paths from L to H:** Any path from L to H must start by traversing either the edge (L, M) or the edge (L, E).

    *   **Paths starting with L-M:**
        *   L-M-F-G-H
        *   L-M-F-X-G-H
        *   L-M-F-K-G-H
        *   L-M-J-I-H
        *   L-M-J-D-P-I-H

    *   **Paths starting with L-E:**
        *   L-E-B-H
        *   L-E-B-N-C-I-H
        *   L-E-B-N-O-P-I-H

4.  **Determine the minimum number of edges for disconnection:**

    *   **Can we disconnect with 1 edge?**
        *   If we remove (L, M), paths starting with L-E-B-H still exist.
        *   If we remove (L, E), paths starting with L-M-...-H still exist (e.g., L-M-F-G-H).
        *   If we remove any other single edge (e.g., (M, F), (E, B), (G, H), etc.), there will always be at least one path remaining from L to H. For example, removing (M, F) leaves L-M-J-I-H and L-E-B-H. Removing (E, B) leaves L-M-F-G-H and L-M-J-I-H.
        *   Therefore, a single edge removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

    *   **Can we disconnect with 2 edges?**
        *   Consider removing the two edges directly connected to L: (L, M) and (L, E).
            *   If (L, M) is removed, all paths starting with L-M are broken.
            *   If (L, E) is removed, all paths starting with L-E are broken.
            *   After removing both (L, M) and (L, E), node L becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between L and H.
        *   This set of two edges, [(L, M), (L, E)], successfully disconnects L from H.

    *   **Alternative cut sets of size 2:**
        *   Let's check if any other set of 2 edges works. For example, consider cutting edges closer to H. The edges directly connected to H are (H, G), (H, B), (H, I). If we remove any two of these, say (H, G) and (H, B), the path L-M-J-I-H still exists. So, removing two edges directly connected to H is not enough. We would need to remove all three (H, G), (H, B), and (H, I) to isolate H, which is 3 edges. Since we found a cut of size 2, this is not minimal.
        *   Consider cutting (M, F) and (E, B). After removing these, paths like L-M-J-I-H still exist. So this is not a valid cut set.

5.  **Conclusion:** Since the minimum number of edges required is at least 2, and we have found a cut set of size 2 that successfully disconnects L from H, this is the minimum.

The final answer is based on the cut set that isolates node L by removing all its outgoing edges.

    *   **Paths starting M -> E:** These paths directly involve E.
        *   Example: M - E - B
    *   **Paths starting M -> K:** Node K is connected to M, X, and F.

    *   The remaining paths, M-K-X-G-H-B and M-K-F-G-H-B, do not necessarily pass through E. Let's call this **Group 2 paths**.

4.  **Formulate a Strategy for Disconnection:**
    *   To disconnect M from B, I must break all paths in Group 1 AND all paths in Group 2.
    *   **Breaking Group 1 Paths:** Removing node E will sever all connections that rely on E, effectively breaking all paths in Group 1 (M-L-E-..., M-E-..., M-K-F-E-..., M-K-X-G-F-E-...).

        *   M - K - F - G - H - B
        To break these two paths, I need to remove a node that is common to both. The common nodes are K, G, and H. Removing any one of these three nodes will break both remaining paths.

5.  **Determine Minimum Cut Set Size:**
    *   If I remove only one node (e.g., E), paths like M-K-X-G-H-B still exist.
    *   If I remove only one node (e.g., H), paths like M-E-B still exist.
    *   No single node removal can disconnect M and B. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.
    *   Based on the strategy above, removing E (to break Group 1 paths) and then one of {K, G, H} (to break Group 2 paths) results in a cut set of size 2.

6.  **Verify a Cut Set of Size 2:**
    *   Let's choose the cut set [E, H].
        *   Removing E breaks all paths that pass through E (all Group 1 paths).
        *   Removing H breaks the remaining paths M-K-X-G-H-B and M-K-F-G-H-B (all Group 2 paths).

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 18
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are N and P. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect N from P. I cannot remove N or P.

2.  **Explore paths from N to P:** I will systematically identify paths to understand the graph's connectivity between N and P.

3.  **Identify N's direct neighbors:** N is connected to Y, O, C, B.
4.  **Identify P's direct neighbors:** P is connected to O, I, E, J, F.

5.  **Attempt to find node-disjoint paths:** According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove (node-cut) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between the two target nodes. Node-disjoint paths share no intermediate nodes.

    *   **Path 1: N - O - P**
        *   Intermediate node: {O}

    *   **Path 2: N - C - I - P**
        *   Intermediate nodes: {C, I}
        *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 (O, C, I are all distinct).

    *   **Path 3: N - B - E - P**
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, E}
        *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 (O, B, E are distinct).
        *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 2 (C, I, B, E are distinct).

    I have found 3 node-disjoint paths. This implies that the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.

6.  **Search for a cut set of size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects N from P.

    Let's consider the direct neighbors of N: {Y, O, C, B}.
    If I remove the nodes {O, C, B}, what happens to N?
    *   N is connected to Y.
    *   N is connected to O (removed).
    *   N is connected to C (removed).
    *   N is connected to B (removed).

    After removing O, C, and B, N is only connected to Y.
    Now, let's check Y. Y is connected to N and O. Since O has been removed, Y is only connected to N.
    This means that N (and Y) are completely isolated from the rest of the graph.
    Therefore, no path can exist from N to P.

7.  **Verify the cut set {O, C, B}:**
    *   If O, C, B are removed, N is left with only Y as a neighbor.
    *   Y is connected to N and O. Since O is removed, Y is only connected to N.
    *   Thus, N is disconnected from all other nodes in the graph, including P.
    *   Each node in the cut set is necessary:
        *   If O is not removed, N-O-P is a path.
        *   If C is not removed, N-C-I-P is a path.
        *   If B is not removed, N-B-E-P is a path.
        *   Removing any two of {O, C, B} would leave the third, allowing a path. For example, removing O and C leaves B, and N-B-E-P is still a path.

8.  **Conclusion:** Since I found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. I have also found a cut set of size 3 ({O, C, B}) that successfully disconnects N from P. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [O, C, B]

    *   This gives us a potential cut set: `[(J, D), (P, D), (Y, D)]` with a size of 3.

3.  **Explore if a smaller cut (e.g., size 2) is possible:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we must demonstrate that no set of 2 edges can disconnect E and D. Let's consider the various ways paths can reach D. All paths to D must pass through one of its direct neighbors: J, P, or Y.

    *   B - N - Y - O - P - J - M (uses (J, M))

*   **Cut Set of Size 1:**

*   **Cut Set of Size 2:**

    2.  Paths that go through node J (and then to M). These paths can originate from B via H, C, or N.

        *   Paths like B - H - E - M are broken.
        *   Paths like B - H - G - F - E - M are broken.
        *   Paths like B - H - E - L - M are broken.
        *   Paths like B - H - G - F - E - L - M are broken.

        *   B - H - I - P - J - M (still exists)

    *   Paths through H and I (leading to J) are cut by (J, M).

*   If only (J, M) is removed, paths like B - H - E - M still exist.

3.  **Check for smaller cut sets (size 1 or 2):**

    *   **Can 1 edge disconnect F and J?**
        A single edge removal would only disconnect F and J if that edge were a bridge connecting the two components containing F and J. By visual inspection, there is no single edge whose removal would disconnect F from J. For example, if we remove (J,M), paths through I (e.g., F-X-G-H-I-J) and P (e.g., F-E-B-C-O-P-J) still exist.     *   **Can 2 edges disconnect F and J?**
        Let's consider different scenarios for removing 2 edges:
        *   **Scenario A: Both edges are connected to J.**
            If we remove any two edges connected to J (e.g., (J, P) and (J, I)), the third edge connected to J (e.g., (J, M)) would still allow paths from F to J (e.g., F-K-M-J). Therefore, removing two edges directly connected to J is not sufficient.

            *   Paths via M are broken by (J, M).
            *   Paths via I: F-X-G-H-I-J, F-G-H-I-J, F-E-B-H-I-J are broken by (H, I). However, the path F-E-B-C-I-J still exists.
            *   Paths via P: F-E-B-C-O-P-J and F-E-B-C-N-O-P-J still exist.
            Since paths still exist, this combination is not sufficient.
            Similar logic applies to other combinations where one edge is connected to J and one is not.
        *   **Scenario C: Neither edge is connected to J.**
            Let's consider two edges that are "bottlenecks" further away from J. For example, (E, M) and (H, I).
            *   Paths via M: F-K-M-J, F-X-K-M-J, F-G-X-K-M-J still exist (as (K,M) is not cut). F-E-M-J is broken by (E,M).

            *   Paths via P: F-E-B-C-O-P-J and F-E-B-C-N-O-P-J still exist (as (O,P) is not cut).
            Since paths still exist, this combination is not sufficient.
            It is difficult to find two edges that would cut all paths to M, I, and P simultaneously without cutting the edges directly connected to J. For example, to cut all paths to M, you would need to cut both (K,M) and (E,M). To cut all paths to I, you would need to cut both (H,I) and (C,I). To cut all paths to P, you would need to cut (O,P). This would require 2+2+1 = 5 edges, which is more than 3.

4.  **Conclusion:**

    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove, I can look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between H and B. If I can find 'k' edge-disjoint paths, then at least 'k' edges must be removed to disconnect the nodes.

    Let's identify some edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B (This is a direct path using the edge (H, B)).
    *   **Path 2:** H - G - F - E - B (This path uses edges (H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, B)).
    *   **Path 3:** H - I - C - B (This path uses edges (H, I), (I, C), (C, B)).

    Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   Path 1 uses (H, B).
    *   Path 2 uses (H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, B). None of these edges are (H, B).
    *   Path 3 uses (H, I), (I, C), (C, B). None of these edges are (H, B), nor are they any of the edges from Path 2.
    Indeed, these three paths are edge-disjoint.

3.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:**
    Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and B, a minimum of 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

4.  **Find a cut set of 3 edges:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect H from B is to remove all edges directly connected to H that lead to B or to other parts of the graph that could eventually lead to B.
    The edges connected to H are (H, B), (H, G), and (H, I).
    *   Removing (H, B) breaks the direct path.
    *   Removing (H, G) breaks all paths that start from H through G (e.g., H-G-F-E-B, H-G-X-F-E-B, H-G-K-M-E-B).
    *   Removing (H, I) breaks all paths that start from H through I (e.g., H-I-C-B, H-I-P-O-C-B, H-I-P-O-N-B, H-I-J-M-E-B).

    By removing these three edges, node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore from node B. This set of edges is: [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)].

5.  **Verify the cut set:**
    *   The direct path H-B is broken by removing (H, B).
    *   Any path starting H-G-... is broken by removing (H, G).
    *   Any path starting H-I-... is broken by removing (H, I).
    Since all paths from H must start with one of these three edges, removing them ensures no path exists between H and B.

This cut set has a size of 3, which matches the minimum required number of edges based on the edge-disjoint paths.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are G and N. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from N.

2.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's theorem states that the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes (the edge-connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. I will attempt to find edge-disjoint paths from G to N.

3.  **Find Edge-Disjoint Paths:**
    *   **Path 1:** G - H - B - N
        Edges used: `(G, H), (H, B), (B, N)`
    *   **Path 2:** G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N
        Edges used: `(G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)`
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 (they share no common edges).
    *   **Path 3:** G - E - H - C - N
        Edges used: `(G, E), (E, H), (H, C), (C, N)`
        Let's verify if Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:
        *   Compared to Path 1: `(G, E)` is distinct from `(G, H)`; `(E, H)` is distinct from `(H, B)`; `(H, C)` is distinct from `(B, N)`. No common edges.
        *   Compared to Path 2: `(G, E)` is distinct from `(G, F)`; `(E, H)` is distinct from `(F, E)` and `(E, M)`; `(H, C)` is distinct from `(M, J)` etc. No common edges.
    Therefore, we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and N.

4.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, by Menger's theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect G and N.

5.  **Identify a Minimal Cut Set:** Now, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects G and N.
    Consider the set of nodes {G, K, X, F}. All paths from G to N must originate from G and eventually leave this local cluster. The edges connecting this cluster to the rest of the graph are:
    *   (G, E)
    *   (G, H)
    *   (F, E)
    If these three edges are removed, the component {G, K, X, F} becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Since N is not part of this isolated component, G and N are disconnected.

6.  **Verify the Cut Set:** Removing `[(G, E), (G, H), (F, E)]` ensures that no path can exist from G (or any node in its immediate cluster {G, K, X, F}) to E or H, which are the only gateways to the rest of the graph leading towards N. Thus, G is completely disconnected from N.

7.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of edges to remove is 3, and one such cut set is `[(G, E), (G, H), (F, E)]`.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, E), (G, H), (F, E)]

Image name: POGBa_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 18
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are M (black) and B (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between M and B.

1.  **Identify direct connections of target nodes:**
    *   Node M is connected to: E, L, K.
    *   Node B is connected to: E, H, C, N.

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate M, I can remove all edges connected to it: (M, E), (M, L), (M, K). This is a cut set of size 3. If these edges are removed, M cannot reach any other node, including B.
    *   To isolate B, I can remove all edges connected to it: (B, E), (B, H), (B, C), (B, N). This is a cut set of size 4.

3.  **Determine if a cut of size 1 or 2 is possible:**
    *   **Size 1:** Removing any single edge will not disconnect M and B. For example, removing (M, E) still leaves paths like M-L-E-B or M-K-X-G-H-B. Removing (E, B) still leaves paths like M-K-X-G-H-B or M-K-X-G-H-I-C-B. Thus, a single edge is insufficient.
    *   **Size 2:** Let's systematically check if any two edges can disconnect M and B.
        *   **Case A: Both edges are connected to M.**
            *   If I remove (M, E) and (M, L), a path M-K-...-B still exists (e.g., M-K-X-G-H-B).
            *   If I remove (M, E) and (M, K), a path M-L-E-B still exists.
            *   If I remove (M, L) and (M, K), a path M-E-B still exists.
            Therefore, two edges connected to M are not enough.
        *   **Case B: Both edges are connected to B.**
            *   If I remove (E, B) and (H, B), paths through C and N still exist (e.g., M-K-X-G-H-I-C-B, M-K-X-G-H-I-C-N-B).
            *   Similarly, any pair of edges from {(E, B), (H, B), (C, B), (N, B)} will leave at least two other paths to B open.
            Therefore, two edges connected to B are not enough.
        *   **Case C: One edge connected to M, one edge connected to B.**
            *   Let's try removing (M, E) and (E, B).
                *   Paths through E are cut.
                *   However, paths from M through K (e.g., M-K-X-G-H-B) are still open.
            *   Let's try removing (M, K) and (H, B).
                *   Paths from M through K to H-B are cut.
                *   However, paths from M through E (e.g., M-E-B) are still open.
                *   Paths from M through K to C-B or N-B (e.g., M-K-X-G-H-I-C-B) are still open.
            It appears that no combination of two edges can completely disconnect M and B.

4.  **Conclusion on minimum cut size:**
    Since a cut of size 2 is not possible, and a cut of size 3 (by isolating M) is possible, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

5.  **Identify a valid cut set of size 3:**
    The simplest cut set is to remove all edges directly connected to M:
    *   (M, E)
    *   (M, L)
    *   (M, K)
    Removing these three edges ensures that M has no connection to any other node in the graph, thus disconnecting it from B.

    Alternatively, another valid cut set of size 3 is:
    *   (E, B) - Cuts all paths through E to B.
    *   (G, H) - Cuts all paths from the M-side (via K, X, F) to H, which then leads to B.
    *   (I, C) - Cuts all paths from the M-side (via K, X, F, G, H) to C, which then leads to B (either directly or via N).

    Both cut sets are valid and have a size of 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, E), (M, L), (M, K)]

Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 18
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are H and O. My task is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H and O. I cannot remove H or O.

First, I will identify all possible paths from H to O.
The direct neighbors of H are G, F, E, B, I.
The direct neighbors of O are C, N, P, Y.

Let's list the paths, grouping them by the first node from H:

Paths starting with H - B:
1. H - B - C - O
2. H - B - N - O

Paths starting with H - I:
3. H - I - C - O
4. H - I - P - O
5. H - I - J - P - O

Paths starting with H - E:
6. H - E - I - C - O (uses I, C)
7. H - E - I - P - O (uses I, P)
8. H - E - I - J - P - O (uses I, J, P)
9. H - E - M - J - P - O (uses M, J, P)
10. H - E - L - M - J - P - O (uses L, M, J, P)

Paths starting with H - F or H - G:
- H - F - E - ... (all these paths will eventually go through E, and then follow the paths listed under H - E)
- H - G - F - E - ... (all these paths will eventually go through E, and then follow the paths listed under H - E)
- H - G - X - F - E - ... (all these paths will eventually go through E, and then follow the paths listed under H - E)

From this analysis, it's clear that all paths from H to O must pass through at least one of the nodes B, I, or E. If we remove these three nodes, H will be disconnected from O.
Let's verify this:
If nodes B, I, and E are removed:
- H is connected to G, F, X, K.
- O is connected to C, N, P, Y.
- The remaining neighbors of H (G, F) are only connected to each other and X, K.
- F was connected to E (removed).
- G was connected to F (which connected to E, removed).
- X and K are connected to F and G, which no longer have a path to O.
Therefore, removing {B, I, E} successfully disconnects H from O. This is a cut set of size 3.

Now, I need to determine if this is the minimum number of nodes. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two nodes (a vertex cut) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them. If I can find 3 node-disjoint paths, then 3 is indeed the minimum.

Let's try to find three node-disjoint paths between H and O (excluding H and O themselves):
Path 1: H - B - N - O
    Nodes in path (excluding H, O): {B, N}
Path 2: H - I - C - O
    Nodes in path (excluding H, O): {I, C}
Path 3: H - E - M - J - P - O
    Nodes in path (excluding H, O): {E, M, J, P}

Let's check if these three sets of intermediate nodes are disjoint:
- {B, N}
- {I, C}
- {E, M, J, P}

These three sets are indeed mutually disjoint. This means we have found 3 node-disjoint paths between H and O.
By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect H and O is 3.
Since we found a cut set of size 3 ({B, I, E}), this is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, I, E]

Image name: PGS_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 18
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between H and N.

1.  **Identify direct neighbors of H and N:**
    *   Neighbors of H: G, B, I
    *   Neighbors of N: B, C, Y, O

2.  **Explore potential paths between H and N:**
    I will look for edge-disjoint paths from H to N. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum edge cut size is at least *k*.

    Let's identify three distinct paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        Edges: (H, B), (B, N)
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        Edges: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   **Path 3:** H - G - K - J - P - O - N
        Edges: (H, G), (G, K), (K, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)

3.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    *   Path 1 uses edges (H, B) and (B, N).
    *   Path 2 uses edges (H, I), (I, C), and (C, N).
    *   Path 3 uses edges (H, G), (G, K), (K, J), (J, P), (P, O), and (O, N).

    Upon inspection, all edges in Path 1 are distinct from those in Path 2 and Path 3. Similarly, all edges in Path 2 are distinct from Path 3. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut size:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, according to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and N is at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal will disconnect H and N.

    *   **Option A: Disconnect H from all its neighbors.**
        If I remove all edges directly connected to H, H will be isolated.
        Edges: (H, G), (H, B), (H, I)
        This set has 3 edges.

    *   **Option B: Disconnect N from all relevant incoming paths.**
        N is connected to B, C, Y, and O. Node Y is a leaf node connected only to N, and no path from H can reach Y without passing through N. Therefore, (N, Y) is not an edge that needs to be cut to disconnect H from N. All paths from H to N must pass through B, C, or O.
        If I remove the edges connecting N to B, C, and O, N will be disconnected from all paths originating from H.
        Edges: (B, N), (C, N), (O, N)
        This set also has 3 edges.

    Both options provide a valid cut set of size 3. I will choose Option B.

6.  **Verify the chosen cut set:**
    If edges (B, N), (C, N), and (O, N) are removed:
    *   Any path H - ... - B - N is broken by removing (B, N).
    *   Any path H - ... - C - N is broken by removing (C, N).
    *   Any path H - ... - O - N is broken by removing (O, N).
    Since all paths from H to N must pass through B, C, or O, removing these three edges guarantees that no path exists between H and N.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, N), (C, N), (O, N)]

Image name: FD-FR_task2_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 18
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are N and P.
2.  **Goal:** Find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect N and P, without removing N or P.
3.  **Systematic Path Exploration (Menger's Theorem Intuition):** To find the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between N and P. If there are `k` node-disjoint paths, then at least `k` nodes must be removed to disconnect the two target nodes.

    Let's identify node-disjoint paths (paths that do not share any intermediate nodes):
    *   **Path 1:** N - O - P
        Intermediate node: {O}
    *   **Path 2:** N - C - I - P
        Intermediate nodes: {C, I}
    *   **Path 3:** N - B - H - G - F - E - M - J - P
        Intermediate nodes: {B, H, G, F, E, M, J}

4.  **Verify Node-Disjoint Property:**
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {O}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {C, I}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {B, H, G, F, E, M, J}.

    These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually disjoint. This confirms that there are at least 3 node-disjoint paths between N and P. Therefore, according to Menger's theorem, a minimum of 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect N and P.

5.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects N and P. Based on the identified paths, a cut set must include:
    *   One node from Path 1 (e.g., O).
    *   One node from Path 2 (e.g., C or I).
    *   One node from Path 3 (e.g., B, H, G, F, E, M, or J).

    Let's try the cut set {O, C, B}:
    *   **Remove O:** This breaks Path 1 (N-O-P) and any path going through Y (N-Y-O-P).
    *   **Remove C:** This breaks Path 2 (N-C-I-P and N-C-I-J-P).
    *   **Remove B:** This breaks Path 3 (N-B-H-G-F-E-M-J-P) and all other paths originating from N through B (e.g., N-B-H-I-P, N-B-H-I-J-P, N-B-H-G-F-E-I-P, N-B-H-G-F-E-I-J-P).

6.  **Verify Disconnection:** After removing O, C, and B:
    *   N's direct neighbors B, C, O are removed.
    *   N's only remaining neighbor is Y. Y is connected only to O, which has been removed. Therefore, N cannot reach any other node via Y.
    *   Since all paths from N through B, C, O, and Y are severed, N is completely disconnected from P.

7.  **Conclusion:** A cut set of size 3 has been found, and it has been established that at least 3 nodes are required. Therefore, 3 is the minimum.

Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [O, C, B]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['O', 'I', 'B'], ['O', 'I', 'E'], ['O', 'I', 'M']]}

Image name: Tu_task3_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 18
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect F from J.

First, I identify the immediate neighbors of F and J to understand their local connections:
*   Neighbors of F: G, K, X, P
*   Neighbors of J: P, D, M, I

Next, I systematically identify all possible paths from F to J. I can categorize these paths based on the first step taken from F.

**Category 1: Paths starting with F - P**
1.  F - P - J (Direct path)
2.  F - P - I - J
3.  F - P - D - J
4.  F - P - E - M - J

**Category 2: Paths starting from F through G, K, or X**
Nodes G, K, and X form a cluster with F (F-G, F-K, F-X, G-K, G-X, K-X). To leave this cluster and reach J, any path must use an edge connecting to a node outside this cluster.
*   The only edge connecting from {F, G, K, X} to the rest of the graph (excluding (F, P) which is in Category 1) is (G, H).
*   Therefore, any path from F that does not go through P must go through G, and then through H.
    *   F - G - H - I - J
    *   F - G - H - I - P - J (This path eventually uses P, but it starts via G-H)
    *   F - G - H - I - P - D - J
    *   F - G - H - I - P - E - M - J

Now, I analyze the "bottleneck" edges that all paths must traverse.
All paths from F to J must pass through one of two critical "entry points" from F's immediate neighborhood to the rest of the graph:
1.  The edge (F, P)
2.  The edge (G, H) (as G is the only node in {G, K, X} that connects to the rest of the graph via H)

Let's test removing these two edges:
*   If I remove the edge (F, P): All paths in Category 1 are broken. However, paths in Category 2 (e.g., F - G - H - I - J) would still exist.
*   If I remove the edge (G, H): All paths in Category 2 are broken. However, paths in Category 1 (e.g., F - P - J) would still exist.

Since removing either (F, P) or (G, H) alone is not sufficient to disconnect F from J, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.

Now, let's consider removing both (F, P) and (G, H):
*   Removing (F, P) breaks all paths that start by going from F to P.
*   Removing (G, H) breaks all paths that start by going from F to G (and then to H).
*   Since all paths from F to J must fall into one of these two categories, removing both (F, P) and (G, H) will break ALL paths between F and J.
*   After removing these two edges, the node F (along with G, K, X) becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, including J.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, P), (G, H)]
